# Summary

The change adds a command-line parameter registry, typed parsers, user-facing kernel-parameter docs, and an early serial fallback for logging when no console device is registered. The broad shape is useful, especially the macro-based registration and the move toward documented boot parameters.

The top issues are correctness and documentation mismatches: unknown init arguments are reordered and deduplicated, grouped Linux CPU-list syntax is implemented with the wrong semantics, and the new early logger fallback performs serial output while holding the console-device-table spinlock. The docs also overstate repeated `console=` support and omit the newly consumed-but-unimplemented Linux parameters. The API surface would benefit from tightening public registry/parser internals before downstream users can depend on them.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/Cargo.toml` line 13

> ```diff
> inventory = { git = "https://github.com/asterinas/inventory", rev = "9dce587" }
> ```

`workspace-deps` (minor): `inventory` is added as a direct dependency of `aster-cmdline` instead of being declared in the workspace dependency table and referenced with `.workspace = true`, which breaks the repository-wide dependency convention.

**Fix.** Move the `inventory` declaration to `[workspace.dependencies]` in `Cargo.toml`, then use `inventory.workspace = true` in `kernel/comps/cmdline/Cargo.toml`.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 134

> ```diff
> /// Defines an **early** `key=value` kernel command-line parameter.
> ///
> /// Almost same as [`define_flag_param!`], but it is processed earlier in the boot sequence.
> ```

Inaccurate documentation (minor): `define_flag_param_early!` is documented as an early `key=value` parameter, but it expands to `setup_flag_param` and accepts bare flags too. The public macro docs disagree with `define_flag_param!` and the parser contract.

**Fix.** Shared with the Documentation comment for `define_flag_param_early!`: change the first sentence to describe an early flag parameter, for example `Defines an **early** flag kernel command-line parameter.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 179

> ```diff
> pub fn argv(&self) -> &Vec<CString> {
>     &self.argv
> }
> 
> pub fn envp(&self) -> &Vec<CString> {
>     &self.envp
> }
> ```

`information-hiding` (minor): `InitprocArgs::argv` and `InitprocArgs::envp` expose the storage as `&Vec<CString>`, so callers learn and depend on the concrete container even though they only need a read-only sequence.

**Fix.** Return slices instead: make `argv` return `&[CString]` and `envp` return `&[CString]`. Existing callers can still use `.to_vec()` on the slice.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 229

> ```diff
> /// Parse a single-value key-value parameter (e.g., log_level=3).
> pub trait ParseParamValue: Sized {
> ...
> /// Parse a repeatable key-value parameter (e.g., "console=ttyS0 console=ttyS1").
> pub trait ParseRepeatableParamValue: Sized {
> ...
> /// Parse a flag parameter (e.g., "ro", "debug", "nokaslr").
> pub trait ParseFlag: Sized {
> ```

`rfc1574-summary` (nit): The trait docs for `ParseParamValue`, `ParseRepeatableParamValue`, and `ParseFlag` use function-style imperative summaries like `Parse ...`; trait summaries should be noun phrases.

**Fix.** Rewrite the first lines as noun phrases, for example `/// A parser for single-value key-value parameters.` and `/// A parser for flag parameters.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 283

> ```diff
> pub struct KernelParam {
>     pub name: &'static str,
>     pub setup: fn(occurrences: &[(&str, Option<&str>)]),
>     pub early: bool,
> }
> ```

`information-hiding` (major): `KernelParam` exposes the registry record (`name`, `setup`, and `early`) as public fields even though users are meant to register parameters through the `define_*_param!` macros. That freezes the internal `inventory` representation into the public API.

**Fix.** Keep the fields private and give the macros a hidden constructor such as `#[doc(hidden)] pub const fn new(...) -> KernelParam`; update the macro expansions to call that constructor.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 8

> ```diff
> #![allow(dead_code)]
> ```

`narrow-lint-suppression` (minor): `#![allow(dead_code)]` suppresses `dead_code` for the whole `types` module, including future private helpers that are unrelated to the current unused items.

**Fix.** Remove the module-wide suppression, or put `#[expect(dead_code)]` only on the specific items that must exist before their first consumer lands.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 31

> ```diff
> pub struct CpuListSegment {
>     pub start: u32,
>     pub end: u32,
>     /// Step within range, default 1.
>     pub stride: u32,
>     /// Optional group size (the `/N` part). When present, stride selection is
>     /// applied within each group window.
>     pub group: Option<u32>,
> }
> ```

`rust-type-invariants` (major): `CpuListSegment` has public fields even though `CpuList` relies on invariants such as `start <= end`, `stride != 0`, and `group != Some(0)`. External code can construct invalid `CpuListSegment` values that the type name suggests should already be valid parsed segments.

**Fix.** Make the fields private, expose getters, and add a validating constructor if external construction is needed.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 73

> ```diff
> let push_or_done = |out: &mut Vec<u32>, cpu: u32| -> bool {
> ```

`closure-fn-suffix` (nit): `push_or_done` holds a closure but does not use the required `_fn` suffix, so the call sites read like a data value rather than a callable helper.

**Fix.** Rename it to `push_or_done_fn`, or extract it into a small helper function.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 112

> ```diff
> let g = s.group.unwrap();
> let stride = s.stride;
> 
> let mut gb: u32 = 0;
> while let Some(v) = s.start.checked_add(gb) {
>     let group_start = v;
> ```

`descriptive-names` (minor): The grouped expansion uses terse names like `s`, `g`, `gb`, and `v` in nested arithmetic over CPU ranges; readers have to decode that `g` is the group size and `gb` is the group base offset.

**Fix.** Rename the locals to semantic names such as `segment`, `group_size`, `group_base_offset`, and `group_start` throughout the loop.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 270

> ```diff
> Some('K') => 1024u64,
> Some('M') => 1024u64.pow(2),
> Some('G') => 1024u64.pow(3),
> Some('T') => 1024u64.pow(4),
> Some('P') => 1024u64.pow(5),
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): `1024` is repeated as the binary unit multiplier for all `MetricU64` suffixes, so the external unit policy is encoded as bare literals at each match arm.

**Fix.** Introduce a named constant such as `const BINARY_UNIT: u64 = 1024;` and derive the suffix multipliers from it.

## Correctness

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 412

> ```diff
> let mut grouped: BTreeMap<String, Vec<(&str, Option<&str>)>> = BTreeMap::new();
> ...
> for (name, occurrences) in &grouped {
>     if let Some(param) = registry.get(name.as_str()) {
>         recognized.push((*param, occurrences));
>     } else {
>         ...
>     }
> }
> ```

Incorrect argument forwarding (major): `dispatch_params()` forwards unknown init arguments by iterating the `BTreeMap` groups, which sorts by normalized key and keeps only the last occurrence in each group. A command line such as `init=/bin/app zarg aarg` now reaches `init` as `aarg zarg`, and `init=/bin/app arg arg` reaches it only once, instead of preserving the command-line `argv` order and multiplicity.

**Fix.** Preserve unknown non-`=` tokens in scan order. For example, build the registry before tokenization, group only recognized parameters for setup, and push unrecognized init `argv`/`envp` entries directly as each token is scanned.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 136

> ```diff
> let g = s.group.unwrap();
> let stride = s.stride;
> ...
> match within.checked_add(stride) {
>     Some(n) => within = n,
>     None => break,
> }
> ```

Incorrect grouped CPU list semantics (major): `expand_bounded()` treats the `:2/25` part of Linux CPU lists as a stride of `2` within each group. Linux's `100-2000:2/25` means “take `2` CPUs from each group of `25`”, so the first values should be `100, 101, 125, 126, ...`; this implementation returns `100, 102, 104, ...`.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: model grouped segments as `used_size/group_size`, iterate group starts by `group_size`, and emit offsets `0..used_size` within each group, clipped by `end`.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 167

> ```diff
> let in_group_offset = offset % g;
> in_group_offset.is_multiple_of(s.stride)
> ```

Incorrect grouped CPU list semantics (major): `contains()` has the same grouped CPU-list interpretation error: for `CpuList::parse_param("100-2000:2/25")`, it reports `102` as included and `101` as excluded, but Linux grouped lists include the first `2` CPUs of each `25`-CPU group.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: change grouped membership to `offset % group_size < used_size`, while keeping plain ranges as arithmetic progressions only when no `/group_size` part is present.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 274

> ```diff
> Some('T') => 1024u64.pow(4),
> Some('P') => 1024u64.pow(5),
> _ => return Err(ParamError::InvalidValue),
> ```

Unsupported valid input (minor): `MetricU64::parse_param()` rejects the Linux metric suffix `E`, so a value such as `1E` is treated as invalid even though Linux-style kernel parameters accept exbibyte units.

**Fix.** Add `Some('E') => 1024u64.pow(6)` and keep the existing `checked_mul()` overflow handling so oversized `E` values still return `ParamError::InvalidValue`.

### `kernel/comps/logger/src/console.rs` line 29

> ```diff
> let devices = aster_console::all_devices_lock();
> ...
> if self.0.is_empty() {
>     ostd::early_print!("{}", s);
> } else {
>     for console in self.0.values() {
>         console.send(s.as_bytes());
>     }
> }
> ```

`no-io-under-spinlock` (major): `_print()` holds the `aster_console::all_devices_lock()` spinlock while calling `ostd::early_print!()`. `early_print()` locks the serial port and spins in UART output, so logging before any console is registered now performs blocking I/O while another spinlock is held.

**Fix.** Check the empty-console case before constructing `Printer`, drop the console-device-table guard, and then call `ostd::console::early_print(args)` outside the spinlock.

## Documentation

### `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` line 24

> ```diff
> book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md:
> 22 Select console devices for kernel messages.
> 23 This parameter may be specified multiple times.
> 24 Kernel messages are delivered to each listed console.
> 
> kernel/src/device/tty/device.rs:
> 105 // TODO: Support specifying multiple TTY devices, e.g., "console=hvc0 console=tty0".
> 106 let console_name = CONSOLES
> 107     .get()
> 108     .and_then(|consoles| consoles.first().map(|s| s.as_str()))
> ```

Incorrect documentation (major): `kernel-parameters.md` says repeated `console=` values deliver kernel messages to each listed console, but the changed TTY code still selects only `CONSOLES.first()` and even keeps a `TODO` for multiple TTY devices. The compatibility document overstates the supported `console=` behavior.

**Fix.** Either implement registration of every listed `console=` device before keeping this wording, or change the document to state that Asterinas currently uses only the first `console=` value and ignores later ones.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 134

> ```diff
> 134 /// Defines an **early** `key=value` kernel command-line parameter.
> 135 ///
> 136 /// Almost same as [`define_flag_param!`], but it is processed earlier in the boot sequence.
> 137 #[macro_export]
> 138 macro_rules! define_flag_param_early {
> ```

Incorrect documentation (minor): `define_flag_param_early!` is documented as defining an early `key=value` parameter, but the macro delegates to `setup_flag_param` and accepts the same bare-token or optional-value flag syntax as `define_flag_param!`. The public macro docs contradict the documented flag API.

**Fix.** Shared with the Maintainability comment for `define_flag_param_early!`: change the first sentence to describe an early flag parameter, for example `Defines an **early** flag kernel command-line parameter.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 473

> ```diff
> 470 // Placeholders for recognized but unimplemented kernel command-line parameters; these
> 471 // consume matching parameters so they are not forwarded to init and avoid unexpected
> 472 // boot behavior. A warning is emitted when such parameters appear.
> 473 define_unimplemented_param!(
> 474     "tsc",
> 475     "no_timer_check",
> 476     "reboot",
> 477     "pci",
> 478     "debug",
> 479     "panic",
> 480     "nr_cpus",
> 481     "selinux",
> 482     "initrd"
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): This change registers `tsc`, `no_timer_check`, `reboot`, `pci`, `debug`, `panic`, `nr_cpus`, `selinux`, and `initrd` as recognized kernel parameters that are consumed and warned about, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` has no entries for them. The Linux compatibility docs therefore do not describe these new user-visible command-line behaviors, especially that these Linux parameters are recognized but not implemented and are not forwarded to `init`.

**Fix.** Add a `Recognized but not implemented` section to `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` listing these parameters and explaining that Asterinas consumes them, logs a warning, and currently applies no Linux behavior for them; alternatively, defer registering them until the documented behavior is ready.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 21

> ```diff
> 16 /// Linux-style CPU list.
> 17 ///
> 18 /// Examples:
> 19 /// - `"1"`
> 20 /// - `"1,2,10-20"`
> 21 /// - `"100-2000:2/25"` (range with stride and optional group size)
> ```

Incorrect documentation (minor): `CpuList` is documented as `Linux-style`, but the example describes `100-2000:2/25` as a range with `stride` and optional group size. Linux's documented CPU-list form uses `used_size/group_size`, so this public documentation gives users the wrong meaning for the `:2/25` syntax.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: if this type is meant to match Linux, update the docs, tests, parser terminology, expansion, and membership logic to `used_size/group_size`; otherwise remove the `Linux-style` claim and document the exact Asterinas-specific `stride/group` behavior.

## Retracted by verification

- `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 306: retracted `ostd-log-only` because this worktree exposes the third-party `log` facade from OSTD but does not provide the `ostd::log` module/macros suggested by the generated fix, so the proposed replacement would not compile here.

# Expected defects

# Summary

The change adds a command-line parameter registry, typed parsers, user-facing kernel-parameter docs, and an early serial fallback for logging when no console device is registered. The broad shape is useful, especially the macro-based registration and the move toward documented boot parameters.

The top issues are correctness and documentation mismatches: unknown init arguments are reordered and deduplicated, grouped Linux CPU-list syntax is implemented with the wrong semantics, and the new early logger fallback performs serial output while holding the console-device-table spinlock. The docs also overstate repeated `console=` support and omit the newly consumed-but-unimplemented Linux parameters. The API surface would benefit from tightening public registry/parser internals before downstream users can depend on them.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/Cargo.toml` line 13

> ```diff
> inventory = { git = "https://github.com/asterinas/inventory", rev = "9dce587" }
> ```

`workspace-deps` (minor): `inventory` is added as a direct dependency of `aster-cmdline` instead of being declared in the workspace dependency table and referenced with `.workspace = true`, which breaks the repository-wide dependency convention.

**Fix.** Move the `inventory` declaration to `[workspace.dependencies]` in `Cargo.toml`, then use `inventory.workspace = true` in `kernel/comps/cmdline/Cargo.toml`.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 134

> ```diff
> /// Defines an **early** `key=value` kernel command-line parameter.
> ///
> /// Almost same as [`define_flag_param!`], but it is processed earlier in the boot sequence.
> ```

Inaccurate documentation (minor): `define_flag_param_early!` is documented as an early `key=value` parameter, but it expands to `setup_flag_param` and accepts bare flags too. The public macro docs disagree with `define_flag_param!` and the parser contract.

**Fix.** Shared with the Documentation comment for `define_flag_param_early!`: change the first sentence to describe an early flag parameter, for example `Defines an **early** flag kernel command-line parameter.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 179

> ```diff
> pub fn argv(&self) -> &Vec<CString> {
>     &self.argv
> }
> 
> pub fn envp(&self) -> &Vec<CString> {
>     &self.envp
> }
> ```

`information-hiding` (minor): `InitprocArgs::argv` and `InitprocArgs::envp` expose the storage as `&Vec<CString>`, so callers learn and depend on the concrete container even though they only need a read-only sequence.

**Fix.** Return slices instead: make `argv` return `&[CString]` and `envp` return `&[CString]`. Existing callers can still use `.to_vec()` on the slice.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 229

> ```diff
> /// Parse a single-value key-value parameter (e.g., log_level=3).
> pub trait ParseParamValue: Sized {
> ...
> /// Parse a repeatable key-value parameter (e.g., "console=ttyS0 console=ttyS1").
> pub trait ParseRepeatableParamValue: Sized {
> ...
> /// Parse a flag parameter (e.g., "ro", "debug", "nokaslr").
> pub trait ParseFlag: Sized {
> ```

`rfc1574-summary` (nit): The trait docs for `ParseParamValue`, `ParseRepeatableParamValue`, and `ParseFlag` use function-style imperative summaries like `Parse ...`; trait summaries should be noun phrases.

**Fix.** Rewrite the first lines as noun phrases, for example `/// A parser for single-value key-value parameters.` and `/// A parser for flag parameters.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 283

> ```diff
> pub struct KernelParam {
>     pub name: &'static str,
>     pub setup: fn(occurrences: &[(&str, Option<&str>)]),
>     pub early: bool,
> }
> ```

`information-hiding` (major): `KernelParam` exposes the registry record (`name`, `setup`, and `early`) as public fields even though users are meant to register parameters through the `define_*_param!` macros. That freezes the internal `inventory` representation into the public API.

**Fix.** Keep the fields private and give the macros a hidden constructor such as `#[doc(hidden)] pub const fn new(...) -> KernelParam`; update the macro expansions to call that constructor.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 8

> ```diff
> #![allow(dead_code)]
> ```

`narrow-lint-suppression` (minor): `#![allow(dead_code)]` suppresses `dead_code` for the whole `types` module, including future private helpers that are unrelated to the current unused items.

**Fix.** Remove the module-wide suppression, or put `#[expect(dead_code)]` only on the specific items that must exist before their first consumer lands.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 31

> ```diff
> pub struct CpuListSegment {
>     pub start: u32,
>     pub end: u32,
>     /// Step within range, default 1.
>     pub stride: u32,
>     /// Optional group size (the `/N` part). When present, stride selection is
>     /// applied within each group window.
>     pub group: Option<u32>,
> }
> ```

`rust-type-invariants` (major): `CpuListSegment` has public fields even though `CpuList` relies on invariants such as `start <= end`, `stride != 0`, and `group != Some(0)`. External code can construct invalid `CpuListSegment` values that the type name suggests should already be valid parsed segments.

**Fix.** Make the fields private, expose getters, and add a validating constructor if external construction is needed.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 73

> ```diff
> let push_or_done = |out: &mut Vec<u32>, cpu: u32| -> bool {
> ```

`closure-fn-suffix` (nit): `push_or_done` holds a closure but does not use the required `_fn` suffix, so the call sites read like a data value rather than a callable helper.

**Fix.** Rename it to `push_or_done_fn`, or extract it into a small helper function.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 112

> ```diff
> let g = s.group.unwrap();
> let stride = s.stride;
> 
> let mut gb: u32 = 0;
> while let Some(v) = s.start.checked_add(gb) {
>     let group_start = v;
> ```

`descriptive-names` (minor): The grouped expansion uses terse names like `s`, `g`, `gb`, and `v` in nested arithmetic over CPU ranges; readers have to decode that `g` is the group size and `gb` is the group base offset.

**Fix.** Rename the locals to semantic names such as `segment`, `group_size`, `group_base_offset`, and `group_start` throughout the loop.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 270

> ```diff
> Some('K') => 1024u64,
> Some('M') => 1024u64.pow(2),
> Some('G') => 1024u64.pow(3),
> Some('T') => 1024u64.pow(4),
> Some('P') => 1024u64.pow(5),
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): `1024` is repeated as the binary unit multiplier for all `MetricU64` suffixes, so the external unit policy is encoded as bare literals at each match arm.

**Fix.** Introduce a named constant such as `const BINARY_UNIT: u64 = 1024;` and derive the suffix multipliers from it.

## Correctness

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 412

> ```diff
> let mut grouped: BTreeMap<String, Vec<(&str, Option<&str>)>> = BTreeMap::new();
> ...
> for (name, occurrences) in &grouped {
>     if let Some(param) = registry.get(name.as_str()) {
>         recognized.push((*param, occurrences));
>     } else {
>         ...
>     }
> }
> ```

Incorrect argument forwarding (major): `dispatch_params()` forwards unknown init arguments by iterating the `BTreeMap` groups, which sorts by normalized key and keeps only the last occurrence in each group. A command line such as `init=/bin/app zarg aarg` now reaches `init` as `aarg zarg`, and `init=/bin/app arg arg` reaches it only once, instead of preserving the command-line `argv` order and multiplicity.

**Fix.** Preserve unknown non-`=` tokens in scan order. For example, build the registry before tokenization, group only recognized parameters for setup, and push unrecognized init `argv`/`envp` entries directly as each token is scanned.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 136

> ```diff
> let g = s.group.unwrap();
> let stride = s.stride;
> ...
> match within.checked_add(stride) {
>     Some(n) => within = n,
>     None => break,
> }
> ```

Incorrect grouped CPU list semantics (major): `expand_bounded()` treats the `:2/25` part of Linux CPU lists as a stride of `2` within each group. Linux's `100-2000:2/25` means “take `2` CPUs from each group of `25`”, so the first values should be `100, 101, 125, 126, ...`; this implementation returns `100, 102, 104, ...`.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: model grouped segments as `used_size/group_size`, iterate group starts by `group_size`, and emit offsets `0..used_size` within each group, clipped by `end`.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 167

> ```diff
> let in_group_offset = offset % g;
> in_group_offset.is_multiple_of(s.stride)
> ```

Incorrect grouped CPU list semantics (major): `contains()` has the same grouped CPU-list interpretation error: for `CpuList::parse_param("100-2000:2/25")`, it reports `102` as included and `101` as excluded, but Linux grouped lists include the first `2` CPUs of each `25`-CPU group.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: change grouped membership to `offset % group_size < used_size`, while keeping plain ranges as arithmetic progressions only when no `/group_size` part is present.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 274

> ```diff
> Some('T') => 1024u64.pow(4),
> Some('P') => 1024u64.pow(5),
> _ => return Err(ParamError::InvalidValue),
> ```

Unsupported valid input (minor): `MetricU64::parse_param()` rejects the Linux metric suffix `E`, so a value such as `1E` is treated as invalid even though Linux-style kernel parameters accept exbibyte units.

**Fix.** Add `Some('E') => 1024u64.pow(6)` and keep the existing `checked_mul()` overflow handling so oversized `E` values still return `ParamError::InvalidValue`.

### `kernel/comps/logger/src/console.rs` line 29

> ```diff
> let devices = aster_console::all_devices_lock();
> ...
> if self.0.is_empty() {
>     ostd::early_print!("{}", s);
> } else {
>     for console in self.0.values() {
>         console.send(s.as_bytes());
>     }
> }
> ```

`no-io-under-spinlock` (major): `_print()` holds the `aster_console::all_devices_lock()` spinlock while calling `ostd::early_print!()`. `early_print()` locks the serial port and spins in UART output, so logging before any console is registered now performs blocking I/O while another spinlock is held.

**Fix.** Check the empty-console case before constructing `Printer`, drop the console-device-table guard, and then call `ostd::console::early_print(args)` outside the spinlock.

## Documentation

### `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` line 24

> ```diff
> book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md:
> 22 Select console devices for kernel messages.
> 23 This parameter may be specified multiple times.
> 24 Kernel messages are delivered to each listed console.
> 
> kernel/src/device/tty/device.rs:
> 105 // TODO: Support specifying multiple TTY devices, e.g., "console=hvc0 console=tty0".
> 106 let console_name = CONSOLES
> 107     .get()
> 108     .and_then(|consoles| consoles.first().map(|s| s.as_str()))
> ```

Incorrect documentation (major): `kernel-parameters.md` says repeated `console=` values deliver kernel messages to each listed console, but the changed TTY code still selects only `CONSOLES.first()` and even keeps a `TODO` for multiple TTY devices. The compatibility document overstates the supported `console=` behavior.

**Fix.** Either implement registration of every listed `console=` device before keeping this wording, or change the document to state that Asterinas currently uses only the first `console=` value and ignores later ones.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 134

> ```diff
> 134 /// Defines an **early** `key=value` kernel command-line parameter.
> 135 ///
> 136 /// Almost same as [`define_flag_param!`], but it is processed earlier in the boot sequence.
> 137 #[macro_export]
> 138 macro_rules! define_flag_param_early {
> ```

Incorrect documentation (minor): `define_flag_param_early!` is documented as defining an early `key=value` parameter, but the macro delegates to `setup_flag_param` and accepts the same bare-token or optional-value flag syntax as `define_flag_param!`. The public macro docs contradict the documented flag API.

**Fix.** Shared with the Maintainability comment for `define_flag_param_early!`: change the first sentence to describe an early flag parameter, for example `Defines an **early** flag kernel command-line parameter.`

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/lib.rs` line 473

> ```diff
> 470 // Placeholders for recognized but unimplemented kernel command-line parameters; these
> 471 // consume matching parameters so they are not forwarded to init and avoid unexpected
> 472 // boot behavior. A warning is emitted when such parameters appear.
> 473 define_unimplemented_param!(
> 474     "tsc",
> 475     "no_timer_check",
> 476     "reboot",
> 477     "pci",
> 478     "debug",
> 479     "panic",
> 480     "nr_cpus",
> 481     "selinux",
> 482     "initrd"
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): This change registers `tsc`, `no_timer_check`, `reboot`, `pci`, `debug`, `panic`, `nr_cpus`, `selinux`, and `initrd` as recognized kernel parameters that are consumed and warned about, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` has no entries for them. The Linux compatibility docs therefore do not describe these new user-visible command-line behaviors, especially that these Linux parameters are recognized but not implemented and are not forwarded to `init`.

**Fix.** Add a `Recognized but not implemented` section to `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md` listing these parameters and explaining that Asterinas consumes them, logs a warning, and currently applies no Linux behavior for them; alternatively, defer registering them until the documented behavior is ready.

### `kernel/comps/cmdline/src/types.rs` line 21

> 16 /// Linux-style CPU list.

Incorrect documentation (minor): `CpuList` is documented as `Linux-style`, but the example describes `100-2000:2/25` as a range with `stride` and optional group size. Linux's documented CPU-list form uses `used_size/group_size`, so this public documentation gives users the wrong meaning for the `:2/25` syntax.

**Fix.** Shared with the other grouped CPU-list comments: if this type is meant to match Linux, update the docs, tests, parser terminology, expansion, and membership logic to `used_size/group_size`; otherwise remove the `Linux-style` claim and document the exact Asterinas-specific `stride/group` behavior.

---
name: aster-code-review
description: Review code against Asterinas's persona-keyed coding guidelines and write a Markdown review file. Use when asked to review a Git change (diff mode) or a set of target files (files mode) for defects, or inside a write-test-review loop.

# aster-code-review

Review code against Asterinas's coding guidelines
— the persona-keyed pages under `book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/`
— and write one Markdown review file.
The review is **recall-first**: miss as few real defects as possible.
It makes both objective calls (undeniable bugs) and subjective calls (guideline violations, each grounded in a cited rule).

There are two review modes, both **anchored at the current checkout (HEAD)**:

- **`diff <base>`** — a Git change:
  the **commit series** the branch adds over its merge-base with `<base>`
  — each commit's message *and* its diff,
  so the review covers per-commit intent and commit hygiene, not just the net change.
  (Commit first; uncommitted edits are not reviewed in diff mode.)
- **`files <path[:lines] ...>`**
  — target code: the working-tree contents of the named files,
  optionally narrowed to line ranges.

To review at a specific commit, check it out first (it becomes HEAD).

This skill is agent-agnostic (Claude Code and Codex).
Only step 3's spawn primitive differs between agents;
everything else is identical.

## Interface

However the skill is triggered,
it receives ONE raw argument string that begins with a mode word:

```
diff   <base>              <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
files  <path[:lines] ...>  <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
```

- `<base>` (diff) — **required**,
  any ref or SHA;
  reviews the commit series `merge-base(<base>, HEAD)..HEAD` (each commit's message + diff).
- `<path[:lines] ...>` (files) — **required**, one or more targets in the working tree.
  A target is a path, optionally `path:N-M` or `path:N-M,K-L` (1-based, inclusive);
  repeat a path to add ranges.
  Wrap a path in double quotes to allow spaces or colons (`"a: b.rs":10-20`).
- `<output>` — **required, last positional** (`cp src... dest` style);
  refuse to overwrite unless `--overwrite`.
- `--overwrite` — replace the output file if it already exists.
- `--per-persona-context` — `yes`: fan out one isolated agent per persona (best recall).
  `no`: one combined agent reviews all personas (cheaper, lower recall).
  `auto` (default) currently resolves to `yes`;
  it will become a benchmark-driven heuristic.
  Controls step 3 only.

**Do not pre-split the argument string yourself.**
Pass it verbatim, as a single quoted argument,
to `resolve_target.sh` (step 1)
— the script self-tokenizes (whitespace-split except inside double quotes),
which keeps the parse deterministic and identical across agents.

## Pipeline

Run these steps in order.
Steps 1 and 5 are deterministic scripts;
steps 6–8 use the model.
Create one fresh temporary directory for the whole run with `mktemp -d`
and put the resolved input, meta file, pass prompts, and fragment directory under it.
Do not use fixed paths such as `/tmp/aster-review-frags`;
stale fragments are worse than a hard failure.

The helper scripts live in the skill's own `scripts/` directory,
at `.agents/skills/aster-code-review/scripts/` inside the repository under review.
**Invoke every script by its absolute path**
— the working directory is the code under review (the repo root), *not* the skill directory,
so a bare `scripts/…` is not found (notably on Codex, which runs commands from the repo root; do not `cd` into the skill dir either — `resolve_target.sh` needs the repo as its cwd).
Shells do not persist between commands,
so **set `SKILL` at the start of each command that uses it** (or inline the `$(git rev-parse …)`):

```sh
SKILL="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.agents/skills/aster-code-review"; "$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'
```

The steps below write `$SKILL/scripts/…` as shorthand for that absolute path.

1. **Resolve the target.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'` prints the canonical review input
   — the commit series (each commit's message + diff) in `diff` mode,
   annotated file excerpts in `files` mode;
   save it to a temp file.
   Run it again with `--meta` first (`"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" --meta '<raw args>'`) to get
   `mode=`, `base=`/`files=`, `head=`, `branch=`, `output=`, `overwrite=`, `per_persona_context=`;
   add `date=` (today) and an optional `title=` to make the meta file.
   Pass the raw argument string as a single quoted argument
   so the script's tokenizer sees it intact.
2. **Activate personas.**
   Pick which of the five personas run,
   from the reviewed paths — changed paths in `diff` mode,
   named paths in `files` mode (see *Activation*).
3. **Fan out.**
   Spawn the persona passes (see *Spawning*).
   With `per_persona_context` = `yes` or `auto` (the default),
   run **one isolated PASS per activated persona** — best recall.
   With `no`, run **one combined PASS** over all activated personas in a single context
   — cheaper, lower recall.
   Build each pass's prompt deterministically with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
   — one persona for fan-out, all activated personas for combined
   — and spawn the sub-agent with **that exact text**.
   Each pass returns a JSON array of comments;
   file each under its persona's `<fragdir>/<persona>.json` (the comment's `persona` field says which),
   so step 5 is unchanged.
   The `<fragdir>` must be freshly created for this review,
   and no pass may read or reuse a fragment from an earlier review.
4. **Collect** the per-persona JSON fragments.
5. **Assemble.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/assemble_review.sh" [--overwrite] <meta> <fragdir> <output>` performs the deterministic merge (group by persona in fixed order, sort by file→line, drop exact duplicates *within a persona*, write frontmatter, leave a `<!-- SUMMARY -->` placeholder).
   Pass `--overwrite` only if the user gave it;
   otherwise the script refuses to clobber an existing `<output>`.
6. **Verify** (see *Verification*).
7. **Consolidate** (see *Consolidation*).
8. **Summary.**
   Replace `<!-- SUMMARY -->` with a constructive summary over the final comments:
   what the code does well, the top issues ranked by severity,
   any structural recommendations.

## Activation

A persona runs unless the reviewed paths *provably* contain nothing in its remit (reviewed paths = changed paths in `diff` mode, named paths in `files` mode):

- **maintainability, development, security** — any reviewed code.
- **hardware** — a reviewed path is assembly (`*.S`, `*.asm`),
  an architecture directory (`.../arch/...`), or contains `asm!` / `global_asm!`.
- **documentation** — a reviewed path is `book/`, any `*.md`,
  syscall-coverage files (`*.scml`), or a user-facing API surface (a syscall or kernel parameter).

Activation is path-based and deterministic;
do not use a model to triage (a wrongly-skipped persona is a silent recall hole).

## Pass contract

The shared reviewer contract
— the review methodology and the JSON comment schema every pass must follow
— lives in [`scripts/pass_contract.md`](scripts/pass_contract.md),
inlined as the **stable head** of every pass prompt by `build_pass_prompt.sh` (step 3).
Do not paraphrase it into the prompt yourself:
retyping it breaks the byte-identical prefix the prompt cache relies on (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).
A pass reads only the persona block(s) it is given (selective exposure),
reviews the **REVIEW INPUT** at the foot of the prompt,
and returns a JSON array of comments per that schema.

## Spawning a pass

Build every pass prompt with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
and spawn the sub-agent with its **exact** output
— only a script keeps the prompt's stable head (contract + guideline) byte-identical across passes and reviews,
which is what lets the prompt cache reuse it (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).

**Default (`per_persona_context` = `yes`/`auto`)**
— one pass per activated persona,
each in a CLEAN context with only its own persona block (selective exposure):

- **Claude Code** — spawn a Task sub-agent per persona.
- **Codex** — write each prompt to a file and run the pass with
  `--output-last-message`/`-o`, for example:

  ```sh
  timeout "${ACR_PASS_TIMEOUT_SECONDS:-600}" \
    codex exec -C "$repo" --sandbox read-only \
      -o "$fragdir/$persona.json" \
      - < "$promptdir/$persona.txt" \
      > "$logdir/$persona.log" 2>&1
  ```

  Do **not** redirect `codex exec` stdout to the fragment file;
  stdout contains session logs as well as the final answer.
  The fragment file must contain only the model's final JSON array.
  Validate every fragment with `python3 -m json.tool` before assembly.
  If a pass times out or validation fails, fail closed instead of assembling a
  review from missing, logged, or stale fragments.

**Never spawn a pass by re-running `aster_code_review.sh` / `run_agent.sh`,
nor by re-issuing the skill's own arguments** (e.g. `codex exec … diff <base> <out>`).
A pass is a *reviewer* invocation whose input is the `build_pass_prompt.sh` text
— not another run of this skill.
Re-entering the launcher spawns another orchestrator that spawns another … , an infinite fork bomb;
the launcher now refuses it (`ACR_AGENT_RUNNING`).

Passes are independent;
order does not matter.
Collect each pass's JSON.

**Combined (`per_persona_context` = `no`)**
— build ONE prompt with all activated personas (`"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input> <p1> <p2> ...`)
and run a single pass;
it reviews through every lens at once and tags each comment with its `persona`.
The input and spawn overhead are paid once,
so it is cheaper
— but a larger shared context dilutes focus,
so it finds fewer defects.
The default stays fan-out:
recall comes first,
and `no` is an explicit, opt-in concession.

## Verification (step 6)

For each comment, isolate the key premise it rests on
— especially an external-system fact (Linux/POSIX behaviour, the System V ABI, Rust semantics).
Try to **refute** it:
re-read the cited code and consult an authoritative source.
For guideline findings,
verify against the persona template and the inlined/bundled guideline root used to build the pass prompt.
Do not retract a finding merely because the historical worktree's `book/` copy has older or narrower wording
when the persona template explicitly instructs the check.
Assign a verdict:

- **confirmed** — keep the comment unchanged.
- **uncertain** — keep it, but prefix `problem` with `(unverified) `.
- **refuted** — remove the comment,
  and append it to a `## Retracted by verification` list at the foot of the file with a one-line reason.

Remove **only** on confident refutation;
an unsure check is `uncertain`, not `refuted`.
This is the only step that may remove a comment, and only false positives.

## Consolidation (step 7)

Find clusters of comments that share one root cause or one fix
(e.g. several manual lock/unlock pairs that all want RAII).
For each cluster, write a single unified fix
and repoint each member's `**Fix.**` at it ("Shared with the other `raii` comments: …").
**Never remove a comment**
— every symptom stays at its own location.

## Output format

The assembled file is:
frontmatter (`date`, `mode`, `base` or `files`, `head`, `branch`, optional `title`),
a `# Summary`,
then a `## <section>` per persona in the order **Maintainability, Correctness, Security, Hardware, Documentation** (Correctness is the Development persona's section).

# Summary

<!-- SUMMARY -->

## Maintainability

### `commit f5fc357bb message`

> ```diff
> Refactor file capability execve handling
> ```

`imperative-subject` (nit): The subject mentions the code identifier `execve` as plain prose, but commit subjects should wrap identifiers in backticks.

**Fix.** Use a subject such as `Refactor file capability `execve` handling`.

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 11

> ```diff
> -pub(crate) const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: XattrName<'static> =
> -    XattrName::from_known_valid_full_name(XattrNamespace::Security, "security.capability");
> +pub const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: &str = "security.capability";
> ```

`rust-type-invariants` (minor): `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` used to be an `XattrName<'static>`, but this refactor weakens it to `&str`, so callers now have to rebuild the typed xattr name and rely on `unwrap()` to preserve the namespace invariant.

**Fix.** Keep the semantic type at the constant boundary, for example define `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` as `XattrName<'static>` and compare `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME.full_name()` where a raw string is needed.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/capabilities.rs` line 66

> ```diff
> +    /// Tries to convert two `u32`s to the capability set.
> +    pub fn try_from_lo_hi(lo: u32, hi: u32) -> Result<Self, InvalidCapSetError> {
> +        let bits = lo as u64 | ((hi as u64) << 32);
> +        Self::try_from(bits)
> +    }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` is exposed as `pub`, but the only current caller is `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` inside the same `credentials` module. This widens the `CapSet` API beyond the actual consumer.

**Fix.** Restrict `try_from_lo_hi` to the smallest useful scope, e.g. `pub(super) fn try_from_lo_hi(...)`, unless another module genuinely needs this constructor.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 80

> ```diff
> +                let Ok(permitted) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 1)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> +                let Ok(inheritable) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> ```

`dry` (minor): `FileCapabilities::parse` repeats the same `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` plus `EINVAL` mapping six times across the revision arms. The old helper kept that conversion policy in one place; now any change to unsupported capability handling has to be edited repeatedly.

**Fix.** Restore a small helper, such as `read_capset_le(raw_value, lo_word, hi_word)`, that reads the two words and maps `InvalidCapSetError` to the shared `EINVAL` message once.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> let xattr_name = XattrName::try_from_full_name(SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> match inode.remove_xattr(xattr_name) {
>     Ok(()) => Ok(()),
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, which rechecks the caller's current `Permission::MAY_WRITE`. That breaks valid writes through an already-writable file descriptor after credentials or mode bits change: `InodeHandle::write()` has already verified `Rights::WRITE`, but this extra inode permission check can return `EACCES` even when there is no `security.capability` xattr to clear.

**Fix.** Use a kernel-internal removal path that bypasses DAC write-permission checks for this automatic `security.capability` cleanup, while still ignoring `ENODATA` and `EOPNOTSUPP`.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 46

> ```diff
> let xattr_name =
>     xattr::XattrName::try_from_full_name(xattr::SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> let value_len = match inode.get_xattr(xattr_name, &mut value_writer) {
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()` now calls `inode.get_xattr()`, but the concrete `Ext2Inode::get_xattr()` and `RamInode::get_xattr()` implementations first require `Permission::MAY_READ`. `execve()` only requires execute permission on the executable, so an execute-only file with a `security.capability` xattr now fails before applying its file capabilities.

**Fix.** Restore a kernel-internal xattr read path for `security.capability` that bypasses the DAC read-permission check, and use it from `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()`.

### `kernel/src/process/execve.rs` line 79

> ```diff
> let exec_euid = if elf_file.mode()?.has_set_uid() {
>     elf_file.owner()?
> } else {
>     credentials.euid()
> };
> let capsets_for_exec = credentials.prepare_capsets_for_exec(file_capabilities, exec_euid)?;
> ...
> set_uid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> set_gid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> credentials.update_capsets_for_exec(capsets_for_exec);
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): `capsets_for_exec` is computed from `elf_file.mode()` and `elf_file.owner()` here, but `apply_caps_from_exec()` later calls `set_uid_from_elf()`, which rereads the same mutable inode metadata before applying those precomputed capability sets. If another process changes the executable's setuid bit or owner in between, the new `euid` and the new permitted/effective capability sets are derived from different file states.

**Fix.** Build and apply one exec credential plan from a single inode metadata snapshot. For example, compute the setuid/setgid decisions and `ExecCapSets` together, then have `apply_caps_from_exec()` apply those stored decisions instead of rereading `elf_inode.mode()` and `elf_inode.owner()`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> pub fn clear_file_capability_xattr(inode: &dyn Inode) -> Result<()> {
>     if inode.type_() != InodeType::File {
>         return Ok(());
>     }
> 
>     let xattr_name = XattrName::try_from_full_name(SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
>     match inode.remove_xattr(xattr_name) {
> ```

Stale privilege metadata (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, but the filesystem implementations of `remove_xattr()` perform a caller DAC write check. This helper is used after privilege-invalidating operations such as `chown`, `truncate`, `O_TRUNC`, and writes; if the invalidating operation has already changed metadata and the DAC check then fails, `security.capability` is left attached to a file whose privileges should have been stripped.

**Fix.** Restore an internal no-DAC removal path for `security.capability`, or add a VFS method specifically for privilege invalidation that removes this xattr after validating only filesystem support and xattr existence, not the current task's file write permission.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/credentials_.rs` line 470

> ```diff
>         let file_effective = if (!no_root && exec_euid.is_root())
>             || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
>         {
>             CapSet::all()
>         } else {
>             CapSet::empty()
>         };
> ```

Incorrect capability transformation (critical): `file_effective` treats any post-`setuid` root `exec_euid` as enabling all newly permitted capabilities, even when the executable has `security.capability` metadata and the caller's real UID is non-root. Linux's `capabilities(7)` exception for set-user-ID-root programs with file capabilities says the file capability bits are honored in this case, so a file with `F(permitted)` set but no file effective flag should not start with those capabilities in `P'(effective)`. This grants effective capabilities that the file deliberately left non-effective.

**Fix.** Gate the root effective shortcut the same way the root file-capability exception is modeled: when file capabilities are present and `self.ruid()` is non-root, use only `FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag()` for `F(effective)`. For example:

```rust
let grant_root_effective = !no_root
    && exec_euid.is_root()
    && (!has_file_capabilities || self.ruid().is_root());
let file_effective = if grant_root_effective
    || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
{
    CapSet::all()
} else {
    CapSet::empty()
};
```

    50	impl XattrNamespace {

    52	        const USER_PREFIX: &str = "user.";
    53	        const TRUSTED_PREFIX: &str = "trusted.";
    54	        const SYSTEM_PREFIX: &str = "system.";
    55	        const SECURITY_PREFIX: &str = "security.";
    56	
    57	        if full_name.starts_with(USER_PREFIX) {
    58	            Some(XattrNamespace::User)
    59	        } else if full_name.starts_with(TRUSTED_PREFIX) {
    60	            Some(XattrNamespace::Trusted)
    61	        } else if full_name.starts_with(SYSTEM_PREFIX) {
    62	            Some(XattrNamespace::System)
    63	        } else if full_name.starts_with(SECURITY_PREFIX) {
    64	            Some(XattrNamespace::Security)
    65	        } else {
    66	            None
    67	        }
    68	    }

   258	impl FileLike for InodeHandle {
   259	    fn read(&self, writer: &mut VmWriter) -> Result<usize> {
   260	        if !self.rights.contains(Rights::READ) {
   261	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is not opened readable");
   262	        }
   263	
   264	        let (file_ops, is_offset_aware) = self.file_ops_and_is_offset_aware();
   265	        let status_flags = self.status_flags();
   266	
   267	        if !is_offset_aware {
   268	            return file_ops.read_at(0, writer, status_flags);
   269	        }
   270	
   271	        let mut offset = self.offset.lock();
   272	
   273	        let len = file_ops.read_at(*offset, writer, status_flags)?;
   274	        *offset += len;
   275	
   276	        Ok(len)
   277	    }
   278	
   279	    fn write(&self, reader: &mut VmReader) -> Result<usize> {
   280	        if !self.rights.contains(Rights::WRITE) {
   281	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is not opened writable");
   282	        }
   283	        if reader.remain() > 0 {
   284	            clear_file_capability_xattr(self.path.inode().as_ref())?;
   285	        }
   286	
   287	        let (file_ops, is_offset_aware) = self.file_ops_and_is_offset_aware();
   288	        let status_flags = self.status_flags();
   289	
   290	        if !is_offset_aware {
   291	            return file_ops.write_at(0, reader, status_flags);
   292	        }
   293	
   294	        let mut offset = self.offset.lock();
   295	
   296	        // FIXME: How can we deal with the `O_APPEND` flag if `open_file` is set?
   297	        if status_flags.contains(StatusFlags::O_APPEND) && self.open_file.is_none() {
   298	            // FIXME: `O_APPEND` should ensure that new content is appended even if another process
   299	            // is writing to the file concurrently.
   300	            *offset = self.path.size();
   301	        }
   302	
   303	        let len = file_ops.write_at(*offset, reader, status_flags)?;
   304	        *offset += len;
   305	
   306	        Ok(len)
   307	    }
   308	
   309	    fn read_at(&self, offset: usize, writer: &mut VmWriter) -> Result<usize> {
   310	        let file_ops = self.file_ops_for_positional_io()?;
   311	        if !self.rights.contains(Rights::READ) {
   312	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is not opened readable");
   313	        }

   322	        if !self.rights.contains(Rights::WRITE) {
   323	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is not opened writable");
   324	        }
   325	        if reader.remain() > 0 {

   336	            offset = self.path.size();

   342	    fn ioctl(&self, raw_ioctl: RawIoctl) -> Result<i32> {
   343	        if self.rights.is_empty() {
   344	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is opened as a path");
   345	        }
   346	
   347	        if let Some(ref open_file) = self.open_file {
   348	            return open_file.ioctl(raw_ioctl);
   349	        }

   353	
   354	    fn mappable(&self) -> Result<Mappable> {
   355	        if self.rights.is_empty() {
   356	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is opened as a path");
   357	        }
   358	
   359	        let inode = self.path.inode();
   360	        if let Some(ref page_cache) = inode.page_cache() {
   361	            // If the inode has a page cache, it is a file-backed mapping and
   362	            // we return the VMO as the mappable object.
   363	            Ok(Mappable::Vmo(page_cache.as_vmo().clone()))
   364	        } else if let Some(ref open_file) = self.open_file {
   365	            // Otherwise, it is a special file (e.g. device file) and we should
   366	            // return the file-specific mappable object.
   367	            open_file.mappable()
   368	        } else {
   369	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::ENODEV, "the file is not mappable");
   370	        }
   371	    }

   373	    fn resize(&self, new_size: usize) -> Result<()> {
   374	        if self.rights.is_empty() {
   375	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EBADF, "the file is opened as a path");
   376	        }
   377	        if !self.rights.contains(Rights::WRITE) {

   380

   382	            // FIXME: It's allowed to `ftruncate` an append-only file on Linux.
   383	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EPERM, "can not resize append-only file");
   384	        }
   385	        clear_file_capability_xattr(self.path.inode().as_ref())?;
   386	        self.path.inode().resize(new_size)
   387	    }

   391	    }

   403	            crate::fs::pipe::check_status_flags(new_status_flags)?;
   404	        }

  1355	    fn set_xattr(
  1356	        &self,
  1357	        name: XattrName,

  1359	        flags: XattrSetFlags,
  1360	    ) -> Result<()> {
  1361	        RamXattr::check_file_type_for_xattr(self.typ)?;
  1362	        self.check_permission(Permission::MAY_WRITE)?;
  1363	        self.xattr.set(name, value_reader, flags)
  1364	    }
  1365	
  1366	    fn get_xattr(&self, name: XattrName, value_writer: &mut VmWriter) -> Result<usize> {
  1367	        RamXattr::check_file_type_for_xattr(self.typ)
  1368	            .map_err(|_| Error::with_message(Errno::ENODATA, "no available xattrs"))?;
  1369	        self.check_permission(Permission::MAY_READ)?;
  1370	        self.xattr.get(name, value_writer)
  1371	    }
  1372	
  1373	    fn list_xattr(&self, namespace: XattrNamespace, list_writer: &mut VmWriter) -> Result<usize> {
  1374	        if RamXattr::check_file_type_for_xattr(self.typ).is_err() {
  1375	            return Ok(0);
  1376	        }
  1377	        if self.check_permission(Permission::MAY_ACCESS).is_err() {
  1378	            return Ok(0);
  1379	        }
  1380	        self.xattr.list(namespace, list_writer)
  1381	    }
  1382	
  1383	    fn remove_xattr(&self, name: XattrName) -> Result<()> {
  1384	        RamXattr::check_file_type_for_xattr(self.typ)?;
  1385	        self.check_permission(Permission::MAY_WRITE)?;
  1386	        self.xattr.remove(name)
  1387	    }
  1388	}

   411	    pub(super) fn prepare_capsets_for_exec(
   412	        &self,
   413	        file_capabilities: Option<FileCapabilities>,
   414	        exec_euid: Uid,
   415	    ) -> Result<ExecCapSets> {
   416	        let capsets = self.calculate_capsets_for_exec(file_capabilities, exec_euid);
   417	
   418	        // Linux performs this safety check only for capability-dumb binaries, which use the
   419	        // effective flag to request that every permitted file capability becomes effective.
   420	        let Some(file_capabilities) =
   421	            file_capabilities.filter(|file_capabilities| file_capabilities.has_effective_flag())
   422	        else {
   423	            return Ok(capsets);
   424	        };
   425	
   426	        if !capsets.permitted.contains(file_capabilities.permitted()) {
   427	            return_errno_with_message!(
   428	                Errno::EPERM,
   429	                "the executable requests file capabilities outside the current bounding set"
   430	            );
   431	        }
   432	
   433	        Ok(capsets)
   434	    }

   437	    pub(super) fn update_capsets_for_exec(&self, capsets: ExecCapSets) {
   438	        self.set_permitted_capset(capsets.permitted);
   439	        self.set_effective_capset(capsets.effective);
   440	    }
   441	
   442	    fn calculate_capsets_for_exec(
   443	        &self,
   444	        file_capabilities: Option<FileCapabilities>,
   445	        exec_euid: Uid,
   446	    ) -> ExecCapSets {
   447	        // Reference: The "Transformation of capabilities during execve()" section and
   448	        // the "Capabilities and execution of programs by root" section in
   449	        // <https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/capabilities.7.html>.
   450	        let no_root = self.securebits().no_root();
   451	        let has_file_capabilities = file_capabilities.is_some();
   452	
   453	        // Linux treats root specially when the executable has no file capabilities, or when the
   454	        // real UID is root. The setuid-root + file-capability exception is handled by excluding
   455	        // the `euid == 0` fast path when a file capability xattr is present.
   456	        let grant_root_file_sets =
   457	            !no_root && (self.ruid().is_root() || (!has_file_capabilities && exec_euid.is_root()));
   458	
   459	        let file_permitted = if grant_root_file_sets {
   460	            CapSet::all()
   461	        } else {
   462	            file_capabilities.map_or(CapSet::empty(), FileCapabilities::permitted)
   463	        };
   464	        let file_inheritable = if grant_root_file_sets {
   465	            CapSet::all()
   466	        } else {
   467	            file_capabilities.map_or(CapSet::empty(), FileCapabilities::inheritable)
   468	        };
   469	
   470	        let file_effective = if (!no_root && exec_euid.is_root())
   471	            || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
   472	        {
   473	            CapSet::all()
   474	        } else {
   475	            CapSet::empty()
   476	        };
   477	
   478	        let new_permitted = (self.inheritable_capset() & file_inheritable)
   479	            | (file_permitted & self.bounding_capset())
   480	            | AMBIENT_CAPSET;
   481	
   482	        let new_effective = (file_effective & new_permitted) | (!file_effective & AMBIENT_CAPSET);
   483	
   484	        ExecCapSets {
   485	            permitted: new_permitted,
   486	            effective: new_effective,
   487	        }
   488	    }
   489	
   490	    // For `setregid`, the real GID can *NOT* be set to the old saved-set GID,
   491	    // For `setresgid`, the real GID can be set to the old saved-set GID.
   492	    fn check_gid_perm(
   493	        &self,
   494	        rgid: Option<&Gid>,

   501	        }

   508	            return_errno_with_message!(

   517	            && *egid != self.sgid()
   518	        {
   519	            return_errno_with_message!(
   520	                Errno::EPERM,
   521	                "the new effective GID is not one of the associated GIDs"
   522	            )
   523	        }
   524	
   525	        if let Some(sgid) = sgid

   104	        elf_file,
   105	        thread_name,
   106	        new_vmar,
   107	        &elf_load_info,
   108	        capsets_for_exec,
   109	    );
   110

   112	        ctx.posix_thread
   113	            .ptrace_may_stop_on(PtraceEvent::Exec(former_tid), ctx, user_context);
   114	    } else {
   115	        ctx.posix_thread
   116	            .enqueue_signal(Box::new(KernelSignal::new(SIGKILL)));
   117	    }
   118

   219	
   220	fn wait_other_threads_exit(ctx: &Context) -> Result<()> {
   221	    let is_main_thread = ctx.posix_thread.tid() == ctx.process.pid();
   222	
   223	    let mut tasks = ctx.process.tasks().lock();
   224	    loop {
   225	        if is_main_thread {
   226	            if tasks.as_slice().len() == 1 {
   227	                return Ok(());
   228	            }
   229	        } else if tasks.as_slice().len() == 2 && tasks.has_exited_main() {
   230	            return Ok(());
   231	        }
   232	
   233	        // Wait until any signal comes or any other thread exits.
   234	        let (waiter, waker) = Waiter::new_pair();
   235	
   236	        ctx.posix_thread
   237	            .set_signalled_waker(waker.clone(), PauseReason::Sleep);
   238	        if ctx.has_pending_sigkill() {
   239	            ctx.posix_thread.clear_signalled_waker();
   240	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EAGAIN, "the current thread has received SIGKILL");
   241	        }
   242	
   243	        tasks.set_execve_waker(waker);
   244	        drop(tasks);
   245	
   246	        waiter.wait();
   247	
   248	        ctx.posix_thread.clear_signalled_waker();
   249	
   250	        tasks = ctx.process.tasks().lock();
   251	        tasks.clear_execve_waker();
   252	    }
   253	}
   254	
   255	fn make_current_main_thread(ctx: &Context) {
   256	    let pid = ctx.process.pid();
   257	    let old_tid = ctx.posix_thread.tid();
   258	
   259	    // The current thread is already the main thread.
   260	    if old_tid == pid {
   261	        return;
   262	    }
   263	
   264	    // The current thread is not the main thread.
   265	
   266	    // Lock order: PID table -> tracer.tracees -> tasks of process
   267	    let mut pid_table = pid_table::pid_table_mut();
   268	    let tracer = ctx.posix_thread.tracer();
   269	    let mut tracer_tracees = tracer
   270	        .as_ref()
   271	        .map(|tracer| tracer.as_posix_thread().unwrap().tracees().unwrap().lock());
   272	    let mut tasks = ctx.process.tasks().lock();
   273	
   274	    assert!(tasks.has_exited_main());
   275	    assert!(tasks.in_execve());
   276	    assert_eq!(tasks.as_slice().len(), 2);
   277	    assert!(core::ptr::eq(ctx.task, tasks.as_slice()[1].as_ref()));
   278	
   279	    tasks.swap_main();
   280	    ctx.posix_thread.set_main(pid);
   281	
   282	    if let Some(tracer_tracees) = tracer_tracees.as_mut()
   283	        && let Some(tracee) = tracer_tracees.remove(&old_tid)
   284	    {
   285	        tracer_tracees.insert(pid, tracee);
   286	    }
   287	
   288	    drop(tasks);
   289	    drop(tracer_tracees);
   290	
   291	    let thread = pid_table.take_thread(old_tid).unwrap();
   292	    pid_table.replace_thread(pid, &thread);
   293	}
   294	
   295	fn set_cpu_context(
   296	    thread_local: &ThreadLocal,
   297	    user_context: &mut UserContext,
   298	    elf_load_info: &ElfLoadInfo,
   299	) {
   300	    let supp = thread_local.supp_user_context();
   301	
   302	    // Reset FPU context.
   303	    supp.fpu().set(FpuContext::new());
   304	
   305	    // Reset general-purpose registers.
   306	    *user_context.general_regs_mut() = GeneralRegs::default();
   307	    // Clear the TLS pointer.
   308	    #[cfg(target_arch = "x86_64")]
   309	    {
   310	        supp.fs_base().set(FsBase::default());
   311	        supp.gs_base().set(GsBase::default());
   312	    }
   313	    #[cfg(not(target_arch = "x86_64"))]
   314	    user_context.set_tls_pointer(0);
   315	
   316	    // Set the new instruction pointer to the ELF entry point.
   317	    user_context.set_instruction_pointer(elf_load_info.entry_point as _);
   318	    debug!("entry_point: 0x{:x}", elf_load_info.entry_point);
   319	    // Set the new user-space stack pointer.
   320	    user_context.set_stack_pointer(elf_load_info.user_stack_top as _);
   321	    debug!("user stack top: 0x{:x}", elf_load_info.user_stack_top);
   322	}
   323	
   324	/// Sets the UID and GID in the credentials according to the ELF inode.
   325	///
   326	/// The capabilities will be updated accordingly.
   327	fn apply_caps_from_exec(
   328	    process: &Process,
   329	    credentials: &Credentials<ReadWriteOp>,
   330	    elf_inode: &Arc<dyn Inode>,
   331	    capsets_for_exec: ExecCapSets,
   332	) -> Result<()> {
   333	    set_uid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
   334	    set_gid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
   335	    credentials.update_capsets_for_exec(capsets_for_exec);

    // Read and validate file capabilities before reaching the point where an `execve()` failure
    // must kill the process.
    let current_root_uid = ctx.thread_local.borrow_user_ns().owner_uid()?;

        .filter(|file_capabilities| file_capabilities.applies_to_root_uid(current_root_uid));

        .credentials()

    // Ensure no other thread is concurrently performing exit_group or execve.
    // If such an operation is in progress, return EAGAIN.
    let mut task_set = ctx.process.tasks().lock();
    if task_set.has_exited_group() || task_set.in_execve() {
        return_errno_with_message!(
            Errno::EAGAIN,
            "the process has exited or has already executed a new program"
        );
    }
    task_set.start_execve();

    // Terminate all other threads
    sigkill_other_threads(ctx.task, &task_set);
    drop(task_set);

    let former_tid = ctx.posix_thread.tid();

    // After this point, failures in subsequent operations are fatal: the process
    // state may be left inconsistent and it can never return to user mode.

    let res = do_execve_no_return(
        ctx,
        user_context,
        elf_file,
        thread_name,
        new_vmar,
        &elf_load_info,
        file_capabilities,
    );
    user_context.set_stack_pointer(elf_load_info.user_stack_top as _);
    debug!("user stack top: 0x{:x}", elf_load_info.user_stack_top);

/// Sets the UID and GID in the credentials according to the ELF inode.
///
/// The capabilities will be updated accordingly.
fn apply_caps_from_exec(
    process: &Process,
    credentials: Credentials<ReadWriteOp>,
    elf_inode: &Arc<dyn Inode>,
    file_capabilities: Option<FileCapabilities>,
) -> Result<()> {
    set_uid_from_elf(process, &credentials, elf_inode)?;
    set_gid_from_elf(process, &credentials, elf_inode)?;
    credentials.update_capsets_for_exec(file_capabilities);
    credentials.set_keep_capabilities(false)?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Sets the UID in the credentials according to the ELF inode.
///
/// If the ELF inode has the `set_uid` bit, the effective UID is set to the same value as the ELF
/// inode's UID.
fn set_uid_from_elf(
    current: &Process,
    credentials: &Credentials<ReadWriteOp>,
    elf_inode: &Arc<dyn Inode>,
) -> Result<()> {
    if elf_inode.mode()?.has_set_uid() {
        let uid = elf_inode.owner()?;
        credentials.set_euid(uid);

        current.clear_parent_death_signal();
    }

    // No matter whether the ELF inode has `set_uid` bit, SUID should be reset.
    credentials.reset_suid();
    Ok(())
}

/// Sets the GID in the credentials according to the ELF inode.
///
/// If the ELF inode has the `set_gid` bit, the effective GID is set to the same value as the ELF
/// inode's GID.
fn set_gid_from_elf(
    current: &Process,
    credentials: &Credentials<ReadWriteOp>,
    elf_inode: &Arc<dyn Inode>,
) -> Result<()> {
    if elf_inode.mode()?.has_set_gid() {
        let gid = elf_inode.group()?;
        credentials.set_egid(gid);

        current.clear_parent_death_signal();

codex
The metadata consistency issue is confirmed as introduced by the refactor: the old code recomputed capabilities after `set_uid_from_elf()` applied the then-current setuid state, while the new code precomputes from an earlier metadata read and applies UID/GID from a later read. I’m doing a final pass over the style findings now.

.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:45:├── problems.yaml             # the suite (schema below); every problem cites a checkout `commit`
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:63:  commit: <rev>                   # REQUIRED. the snapshot to check out (detached HEAD):
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:65:                                  #   files mode -> any local commit-ish (e.g. f4e29d67c^).
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:66:  remote: <fetch URL>             # OPTIONAL. where to fetch `commit`; defaults to https://github.com/asterinas/asterinas.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:70:    diff:                         #   diff mode: review `base..HEAD` (each commit's message + diff).
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:71:      base: <rev>                 #     REQUIRED ref relative to the checkout; HEAD^ for a single introducing commit.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:72:    files:                        #   files mode: targets reviewed at `commit` (whole-file is the norm)
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:76:        kind: <kind>              #     REQUIRED: file | commit_message | whole_change
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:91:Prose fields (`source`, `desc`, `fix`, `expectation`) are YAML block scalars and may use Markdown `backticks` around code (literal text in YAML).
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:103:a top-level `commit` on every problem — a full-40-char SHA in `diff` mode,
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:104:any commit-ish in `files` mode — with an *optional* `remote` (default upstream);
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:106:`files` is non-empty; no obsolete `base_commit`;
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:111:A `diff` problem is a single commit reviewed as its diff against its parent,
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:113:— which `diff` mode reviews (per-commit intent and commit hygiene).
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:114:The `commit_message` kind targets that message;
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:117:### Why commit references, not local patches
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:120:— a **redundant copy** of a commit that already lives on GitHub, free to drift from it.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:121:We drop it: a `diff` problem now names the commit by full 40-char SHA plus a `remote` to fetch it from.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:124:- **A full-SHA fetch works even for an unreachable, force-pushed-over commit.**
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:127:- **GitHub does not routinely garbage-collect such commits.**
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:128:  Its docs ([*Removing sensitive data from a repository*][gh-sensitive]) state that after a force-push the commits "may still be accessible"
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:134:**PR-derived** commits are ancestors of `asterinas/asterinas`'s `main`
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:137:**Synthetic** commits (a handcrafted regression, a bad commit message) belong to no branch:
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:143:and that the synthetic commits live in a fork rather than mainline;
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:162:   A later commit that *fixes* a bug an earlier commit introduced is an ideal problem:
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:164:   The problem cites the **introducing commit** by SHA (its `remote` is upstream),
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:167:   Because it is the *introducing* commit,
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:171:   A handcrafted buggy commit (a realistic-looking change onto a sound base + the expected defect),
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:173:   It is hosted as a **dangling** commit on a fork and cited by SHA;
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:179:   For a known fixed bug, set `commit = fixed_by^` (the fix's parent) and review the **whole** file(s) the fix touched.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:185:   This works even when the introducing commit is ancient or otherwise unusable (we anchor on the fix, not the introducer),
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:188:   A `commit` plus named files known to contain a defect,
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:198:when a clean introducing commit was unavailable,
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:199:set the base to the *fixing* commit and used the inverse of the fix as the patch.
.agents/skills/aster-code-review/spec/benchmark.md:211:the two synthetic problems (0007–0008) are hosted as dangling commits (see [*Why commit references, not local patches*](#why-commit-references-not-local-patches)).

    30	/// File capabilities loaded from the `security.capability` xattr.
    31	#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug)]
    32	pub(in crate::process) struct FileCapabilities {
    33	    permitted: CapSet,
    34	    inheritable: CapSet,
    35	    has_effective_flag: bool,
    36	    root_uid: Option<Uid>,
    37	}
    38	
    39	impl FileCapabilities {
    40	    /// Reads file capabilities from an inode's `security.capability` xattr.
    41	    pub(in crate::process) fn read_from_inode(inode: &Arc<dyn Inode>) -> Result<Option<Self>> {
    42	        let mut raw_value = [0u8; XATTR_CAPS_SZ_3];
    43	        let mut value_writer = VmWriter::from(raw_value.as_mut_slice()).to_fallible();
    44	        let xattr_name =
    45	            xattr::XattrName::try_from_full_name(xattr::SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
    46	        let value_len = match inode.get_xattr(xattr_name, &mut value_writer) {
    47	            Ok(value_len) => value_len,
    48	            Err(error) if matches!(error.error(), Errno::ENODATA | Errno::EOPNOTSUPP) => {
    49	                return Ok(None);
    50	            }
    51	            Err(error) => return Err(error),
    52	        };
    53	
    54	        Self::parse(&raw_value[..value_len]).map(Some)
    55	    }
    56

    59	    }

    70	        self.root_uid
    71	            .is_none_or(|stored_root_uid| stored_root_uid == root_uid)
