    84	                    );
    85	                };
    86	                let Ok(inheritable) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, 0) else {
    87	                    return_errno_with_message!(
    88	                        Errno::EINVAL,
    89	                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
    90	                    );
    91	                };
    92	                (XATTR_CAPS_SZ_1, permitted, inheritable, None)
    93	            }
    94	            VfsCapRevision::V2 => {
    95	                let Ok(permitted) =
    96	                    CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 1)?, read_u32_le(raw_value, 3)?)
    97	                else {
    98	                    return_errno_with_message!(
    99	                        Errno::EINVAL,
   100	                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
   101	                    );
   102	                };
   103	                let Ok(inheritable) =
   104	                    CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, read_u32_le(raw_value, 4)?)
   105	                else {
   106	                    return_errno_with_message!(
   107	                        Errno::EINVAL,
   108	                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
   109	                    );
   110	                };
   111	                (XATTR_CAPS_SZ_2, permitted, inheritable, None)
   112	            }
   113	            VfsCapRevision::V3 => {
   114	                let Ok(permitted) =
   115	                    CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 1)?, read_u32_le(raw_value, 3)?)
   116	                else {
   117	                    return_errno_with_message!(
   118	                        Errno::EINVAL,
   119	                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
   120	                    );
   121	                };
   122	                let Ok(inheritable) =
   123	                    CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, read_u32_le(raw_value, 4)?)
   124	                else {
   125	                    return_errno_with_message!(
   126	                        Errno::EINVAL,
   127	                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
   128	                    );
   129	                };
   130	                (
   131	                    XATTR_CAPS_SZ_3,
   132	                    permitted,
   133	                    inheritable,
   134	                    Some(Uid::new(read_u32_le(raw_value, 5)?)),
   135	                )
   136	            }
   137	        };
   138	
   139	        if raw_value.len() != expected_len {
   140	            return_errno_with_message!(
   141	                Errno::EINVAL,
   142	                "file capability xattr length does not match its revision"
   143	            );
   144	        }
   145	
   146	        Ok(Self {
   147	            permitted,
   148	            inheritable,
   149	            has_effective_flag: flags.contains(VfsCapFlags::EFFECTIVE),
   150	            root_uid,
   151	        })
   152	    }
   153	}
   154	
   155	fn parse_revision_and_flags(magic_etc: u32) -> Result<(VfsCapRevision, VfsCapFlags)> {
   156	    let revision_bits = magic_etc & VFS_CAP_REVISION_MASK;
   157	    let Ok(revision) = VfsCapRevision::try_from(revision_bits) else {
   158	        return_errno_with_message!(
   159	            Errno::EINVAL,
   160	            "file capabilities use an unsupported xattr revision"
   161	        );
   162	    };
   163	
   164	    let flags_bits = magic_etc & VFS_CAP_FLAGS_MASK;
   165	    let Some(flags) = VfsCapFlags::from_bits(flags_bits) else {
   166	        return_errno_with_message!(
   167	            Errno::EINVAL,
   168	            "file capabilities contain unsupported flag bits"
   169	        );
   170	    };
   171	
   172	    Ok((revision, flags))
   173	}
   174	
   175	fn read_u32_le(bytes: &[u8], word_index: usize) -> Result<u32> {
   176	    let start = word_index * size_of::<u32>();
   177	    let end = start + size_of::<u32>();
   178	    let Some(word_bytes) = bytes.get(start..end) else {
   179	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "file capability xattr is truncated");
   180	    };
   181	
   182	    let mut word = [0u8; size_of::<u32>()];
   183	    word.copy_from_slice(word_bytes);
   184	    Ok(u32::from_le_bytes(word))
   185	}

    60	    pub fn from_lo_hi(lo: u32, hi: u32) -> Self {
    61	        let bits = lo as u64 | ((hi as u64) << 32);
    62	        Self::from_bits_truncate(bits)
    63	    }

    67	        let bits = lo as u64 | ((hi as u64) << 32);
    68	        Self::try_from(bits)
    69	    }

    71	    /// Converts the capability set to two `u32`s.
    72	    pub fn to_lo_hi(self) -> (u32, u32) {
    73	        (self.bits() as u32, (self.bits() >> 32) as u32)
    74	    }

   130	    /// Opens the `Path` with the given `OpenArgs`.
   131	    ///
   132	    /// Returns an `InodeHandle` on success.
   133	    pub fn open(&self, open_args: OpenArgs) -> Result<InodeHandle> {
   134	        let inode = self.inode().as_ref();
   135	        let inode_type = inode.type_();
   136	        let creation_flags = &open_args.creation_flags;
   137	        let status_flags = &open_args.status_flags;
   138	
   139	        if creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_CREAT)
   140	            && creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_EXCL)
   141	        {
   142	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EEXIST, "the file already exists");
   143	        }
   144	        if creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_CREAT) && inode_type == InodeType::Dir {
   145	            return_errno_with_message!(
   146	                Errno::EISDIR,
   147	                "O_CREAT is specified but the file is a directory"
   148	            );
   149	        }
   150	
   151	        if inode_type == InodeType::SymLink
   152	            && creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_NOFOLLOW)
   153	            && !status_flags.contains(StatusFlags::O_PATH)
   154	        {
   155	            return_errno_with_message!(Errno::ELOOP, "the file is a symlink");
   156	        }
   157	        if creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_DIRECTORY) && inode_type != InodeType::Dir {
   158	            return_errno_with_message!(
   159	                Errno::ENOTDIR,
   160	                "O_DIRECTORY is specified but the file is not a directory"
   161	            );
   162	        }
   163	
   164	        if inode_type.is_regular_file()
   165	            && creation_flags.contains(CreationFlags::O_TRUNC)
   166	            && !status_flags.contains(StatusFlags::O_PATH)
   167	        {
   168	            clear_file_capability_xattr(inode)?;
   169	            self.resize(0)?;
   170	        }
   171	
   172	        InodeHandle::new(self.clone(), open_args.access_mode, *status_flags)
   173	    }

   106	    fn file_ops_for_positional_io(&self) -> Result<&dyn FileOps> {
   107	        if let Some(ref open_file) = self.open_file {

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The change usefully separates file-capability parsing from exec credential application and adds stricter parsing for unsupported capability bits. The highest-risk issues are in the behavior changes around privilege handling: file capabilities for setuid-root executables can become effective when Linux would keep them non-effective, and exec now precomputes capability sets from inode metadata that is later reread for UID/GID changes. The xattr refactor also routes internal `security.capability` reads/removals through public xattr methods, introducing DAC permission checks into exec and write/truncate cleanup paths.
+
+Fix the capability transformation and exec metadata snapshot first, then restore internal no-DAC xattr access for kernel privilege bookkeeping. The remaining maintainability items are smaller API-shape and duplication cleanups.
 
 ## Maintainability

20:> Refactor file capability execve handling
25:**Fix.** Use a subject such as `Refactor file capability `execve` handling`.
133:Stale privilege metadata (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, but the filesystem implementations of `remove_xattr()` perform a caller DAC write check. This helper is used after privilege-invalidating operations such as `chown`, `truncate`, `O_TRUNC`, and writes; if the invalidating operation has already changed metadata and the DAC check then fails, `security.capability` is left attached to a file whose privileges should have been stripped.

# Summary

The change usefully separates file-capability parsing from exec credential application and adds stricter parsing for unsupported capability bits. The highest-risk issues are in the behavior changes around privilege handling: file capabilities for setuid-root executables can become effective when Linux would keep them non-effective, and exec now precomputes capability sets from inode metadata that is later reread for UID/GID changes. The xattr refactor also routes internal `security.capability` reads/removals through public xattr methods, introducing DAC permission checks into exec and write/truncate cleanup paths.

Fix the capability transformation and exec metadata snapshot first, then restore internal no-DAC xattr access for kernel privilege bookkeeping. The remaining maintainability items are smaller API-shape and duplication cleanups.

## Maintainability

### `commit f5fc357bb message`

> ```diff
> Refactor file capability execve handling
> ```

`imperative-subject` (nit): The subject mentions the code identifier `execve` as plain prose, but commit subjects should wrap identifiers in backticks.

**Fix.** Use a subject such as `Refactor file capability `execve` handling`.

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 11

> ```diff
> -pub(crate) const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: XattrName<'static> =
> -    XattrName::from_known_valid_full_name(XattrNamespace::Security, "security.capability");
> +pub const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: &str = "security.capability";
> ```

`rust-type-invariants` (minor): `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` used to be an `XattrName<'static>`, but this refactor weakens it to `&str`, so callers now have to rebuild the typed xattr name and rely on `unwrap()` to preserve the namespace invariant.

**Fix.** Keep the semantic type at the constant boundary, for example define `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` as `XattrName<'static>` and compare `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME.full_name()` where a raw string is needed.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/capabilities.rs` line 66

> ```diff
> +    /// Tries to convert two `u32`s to the capability set.
> +    pub fn try_from_lo_hi(lo: u32, hi: u32) -> Result<Self, InvalidCapSetError> {
> +        let bits = lo as u64 | ((hi as u64) << 32);
> +        Self::try_from(bits)
> +    }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` is exposed as `pub`, but the only current caller is `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` inside the same `credentials` module. This widens the `CapSet` API beyond the actual consumer.

**Fix.** Restrict `try_from_lo_hi` to the smallest useful scope, e.g. `pub(super) fn try_from_lo_hi(...)`, unless another module genuinely needs this constructor.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 80

> ```diff
> +                let Ok(permitted) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 1)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> +                let Ok(inheritable) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> ```

`dry` (minor): `FileCapabilities::parse` repeats the same `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` plus `EINVAL` mapping six times across the revision arms. The old helper kept that conversion policy in one place; now any change to unsupported capability handling has to be edited repeatedly.

**Fix.** Restore a small helper, such as `read_capset_le(raw_value, lo_word, hi_word)`, that reads the two words and maps `InvalidCapSetError` to the shared `EINVAL` message once.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> let xattr_name = XattrName::try_from_full_name(SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> match inode.remove_xattr(xattr_name) {
>     Ok(()) => Ok(()),
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, which rechecks the caller's current `Permission::MAY_WRITE`. That breaks valid writes through an already-writable file descriptor after credentials or mode bits change: `InodeHandle::write()` has already verified `Rights::WRITE`, but this extra inode permission check can return `EACCES` even when there is no `security.capability` xattr to clear.

**Fix.** Use a kernel-internal removal path that bypasses DAC write-permission checks for this automatic `security.capability` cleanup, while still ignoring `ENODATA` and `EOPNOTSUPP`.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 46

> ```diff
> let xattr_name =
>     xattr::XattrName::try_from_full_name(xattr::SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> let value_len = match inode.get_xattr(xattr_name, &mut value_writer) {
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()` now calls `inode.get_xattr()`, but the concrete `Ext2Inode::get_xattr()` and `RamInode::get_xattr()` implementations first require `Permission::MAY_READ`. `execve()` only requires execute permission on the executable, so an execute-only file with a `security.capability` xattr now fails before applying its file capabilities.

**Fix.** Restore a kernel-internal xattr read path for `security.capability` that bypasses the DAC read-permission check, and use it from `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()`.

### `kernel/src/process/execve.rs` line 79

> ```diff
> let exec_euid = if elf_file.mode()?.has_set_uid() {
>     elf_file.owner()?
> } else {
>     credentials.euid()
> };
> let capsets_for_exec = credentials.prepare_capsets_for_exec(file_capabilities, exec_euid)?;
> ...
> set_uid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> set_gid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> credentials.update_capsets_for_exec(capsets_for_exec);
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): `capsets_for_exec` is computed from `elf_file.mode()` and `elf_file.owner()` here, but `apply_caps_from_exec()` later calls `set_uid_from_elf()`, which rereads the same mutable inode metadata before applying those precomputed capability sets. If another process changes the executable's setuid bit or owner in between, the new `euid` and the new permitted/effective capability sets are derived from different file states.

**Fix.** Build and apply one exec credential plan from a single inode metadata snapshot. For example, compute the setuid/setgid decisions and `ExecCapSets` together, then have `apply_caps_from_exec()` apply those stored decisions instead of rereading `elf_inode.mode()` and `elf_inode.owner()`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> pub fn clear_file_capability_xattr(inode: &dyn Inode) -> Result<()> {
>     if inode.type_() != InodeType::File {
>         return Ok(());
>     }
> 
>     let xattr_name = XattrName::try_from_full_name(SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
>     match inode.remove_xattr(xattr_name) {
> ```

Stale privilege metadata (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, but the filesystem implementations of `remove_xattr()` perform a caller DAC write check. This helper is used after privilege-invalidating operations such as `chown`, `truncate`, `O_TRUNC`, and writes; if the invalidating operation has already changed metadata and the DAC check then fails, `security.capability` is left attached to a file whose privileges should have been stripped.

**Fix.** Restore an internal no-DAC removal path for `security.capability`, or add a VFS method specifically for privilege invalidation that removes this xattr after validating only filesystem support and xattr existence, not the current task's file write permission.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/credentials_.rs` line 470

> ```diff
>         let file_effective = if (!no_root && exec_euid.is_root())
>             || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
>         {
>             CapSet::all()
>         } else {
>             CapSet::empty()
>         };
> ```

Incorrect capability transformation (critical): `file_effective` treats any post-`setuid` root `exec_euid` as enabling all newly permitted capabilities, even when the executable has `security.capability` metadata and the caller's real UID is non-root. Linux's `capabilities(7)` exception for set-user-ID-root programs with file capabilities says the file capability bits are honored in this case, so a file with `F(permitted)` set but no file effective flag should not start with those capabilities in `P'(effective)`. This grants effective capabilities that the file deliberately left non-effective.

**Fix.** Gate the root effective shortcut the same way the root file-capability exception is modeled: when file capabilities are present and `self.ruid()` is non-root, use only `FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag()` for `F(effective)`. For example:

```rust
let grant_root_effective = !no_root
    && exec_euid.is_root()
    && (!has_file_capabilities || self.ruid().is_root());
let file_effective = if grant_root_effective
    || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
{
    CapSet::all()
} else {
    CapSet::empty()
};
```

===== EXPECTED DEFECTS =====
# Expected defects

1. location: kernel/src/process/credentials/credentials_.rs (persona: security, grounding: Privilege escalation, severity: critical)
   defect: `calculate_capsets_for_exec` computes `file_effective` with `(!no_root && exec_euid.is_root()) || file_capabilities.has_effective_flag()`. That grants a full effective capability set whenever exec makes the effective UID root, even if the executable also carries file capabilities. Linux treats the setuid-root plus file-capability case specially: for a non-root caller executing such a file, the file capabilities suppress the legacy setuid-root full-capability grant, and only the file-capability effective flag should make the permitted set effective.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that setuid-root must not grant a full effective capability set when file capabilities are present for a non-root caller; only the file effective flag should make the permitted set effective.

2. location: kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs (persona: security, grounding: Incorrect permission check, severity: major)
   defect: `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode` now calls `inode.get_xattr(...)` to read `security.capability`. The filesystem `Inode::get_xattr` implementations, such as ext2, perform a normal `MAY_READ` DAC check before looking up the xattr. That check is inappropriate for exec-time file-capability loading: a file can be executable without being readable, for example mode `0111`, and an execute-only file with no `security.capability` xattr should execute normally. With the new path, the read permission check returns `EACCES` before the xattr lookup can return `ENODATA`, so `execve` rejects such programs.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that loading `security.capability` during `execve` must not require the executable to be readable; execute-only files without file capabilities must reach the xattr absence case and execute successfully.

3. location: kernel/src/process/execve.rs (persona: security, grounding: TOCTOU race, severity: major)
   defect: `do_execve` computes `exec_euid` from `elf_file.mode()` and `elf_file.owner()` before the irreversible exec phase, and `prepare_capsets_for_exec` turns that value into cached `ExecCapSets`. Later, `apply_caps_from_exec` calls `set_uid_from_elf` and `set_gid_from_elf`, which re-read the inode mode, owner, and group before installing the already-computed capability sets. If the executable metadata changes between these reads, the final effective UID/GID and the installed capability sets can be derived from different file states.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that exec capability sets are prepared from one executable mode/owner snapshot while the setuid/setgid transition later re-reads inode metadata, so concurrent metadata changes can make the installed capabilities inconsistent with the final credentials.

# Summary

The change usefully separates file-capability parsing from exec credential application and adds stricter parsing for unsupported capability bits. The highest-risk issues are in the behavior changes around privilege handling: file capabilities for setuid-root executables can become effective when Linux would keep them non-effective, and exec now precomputes capability sets from inode metadata that is later reread for UID/GID changes. The xattr refactor also routes internal `security.capability` reads/removals through public xattr methods, introducing DAC permission checks into exec and write/truncate cleanup paths.

Fix the capability transformation and exec metadata snapshot first, then restore internal no-DAC xattr access for kernel privilege bookkeeping. The remaining maintainability items are smaller API-shape and duplication cleanups.

## Maintainability

### `commit f5fc357bb message`

> ```diff
> Refactor file capability execve handling
> ```

`imperative-subject` (nit): The subject mentions the code identifier `execve` as plain prose, but commit subjects should wrap identifiers in backticks.

**Fix.** Use a subject such as ``Refactor file capability `execve` handling``.

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 11

> ```diff
> -pub(crate) const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: XattrName<'static> =
> -    XattrName::from_known_valid_full_name(XattrNamespace::Security, "security.capability");
> +pub const SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME: &str = "security.capability";
> ```

`rust-type-invariants` (minor): `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` used to be an `XattrName<'static>`, but this refactor weakens it to `&str`, so callers now have to rebuild the typed xattr name and rely on `unwrap()` to preserve the namespace invariant.

**Fix.** Keep the semantic type at the constant boundary, for example define `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME` as `XattrName<'static>` and compare `SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME.full_name()` where a raw string is needed.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/capabilities.rs` line 66

> ```diff
> +    /// Tries to convert two `u32`s to the capability set.
> +    pub fn try_from_lo_hi(lo: u32, hi: u32) -> Result<Self, InvalidCapSetError> {
> +        let bits = lo as u64 | ((hi as u64) << 32);
> +        Self::try_from(bits)
> +    }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` is exposed as `pub`, but the only current caller is `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` inside the same `credentials` module. This widens the `CapSet` API beyond the actual consumer.

**Fix.** Restrict `try_from_lo_hi` to the smallest useful scope, e.g. `pub(super) fn try_from_lo_hi(...)`, unless another module genuinely needs this constructor.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 80

> ```diff
> +                let Ok(permitted) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 1)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> +                let Ok(inheritable) = CapSet::try_from_lo_hi(read_u32_le(raw_value, 2)?, 0) else {
> +                    return_errno_with_message!(
> +                        Errno::EINVAL,
> +                        "file capabilities contain unsupported capability bits"
> +                    );
> +                };
> ```

`dry` (minor): `FileCapabilities::parse` repeats the same `CapSet::try_from_lo_hi` plus `EINVAL` mapping six times across the revision arms. The old helper kept that conversion policy in one place; now any change to unsupported capability handling has to be edited repeatedly.

**Fix.** Restore a small helper, such as `read_capset_le(raw_value, lo_word, hi_word)`, that reads the two words and maps `InvalidCapSetError` to the shared `EINVAL` message once.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/fs/vfs/fs_apis/xattr.rs` line 20

> ```diff
> let xattr_name = XattrName::try_from_full_name(SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> match inode.remove_xattr(xattr_name) {
>     Ok(()) => Ok(()),
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `clear_file_capability_xattr()` now calls `inode.remove_xattr()`, which rechecks the caller's current `Permission::MAY_WRITE`. That breaks valid writes through an already-writable file descriptor after credentials or mode bits change: `InodeHandle::write()` has already verified `Rights::WRITE`, but this extra inode permission check can return `EACCES` even when there is no `security.capability` xattr to clear.

**Fix.** Use a kernel-internal removal path that bypasses DAC write-permission checks for this automatic `security.capability` cleanup, while still ignoring `ENODATA` and `EOPNOTSUPP`.

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/file_capabilities.rs` line 46

> ```diff
> let xattr_name =
>     xattr::XattrName::try_from_full_name(xattr::SECURITY_CAPABILITY_XATTR_NAME).unwrap();
> let value_len = match inode.get_xattr(xattr_name, &mut value_writer) {
> ```

Incorrect permission check (major): `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()` now calls `inode.get_xattr()`, but the concrete `Ext2Inode::get_xattr()` and `RamInode::get_xattr()` implementations first require `Permission::MAY_READ`. `execve()` only requires execute permission on the executable, so an execute-only file with a `security.capability` xattr now fails before applying its file capabilities.

**Fix.** Restore a kernel-internal xattr read path for `security.capability` that bypasses the DAC read-permission check, and use it from `FileCapabilities::read_from_inode()`.

### `kernel/src/process/execve.rs` line 79

> ```diff
> let exec_euid = if elf_file.mode()?.has_set_uid() {
>     elf_file.owner()?
> } else {
>     credentials.euid()
> };
> let capsets_for_exec = credentials.prepare_capsets_for_exec(file_capabilities, exec_euid)?;
> ...
> set_uid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> set_gid_from_elf(process, credentials, elf_inode)?;
> credentials.update_capsets_for_exec(capsets_for_exec);
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): `capsets_for_exec` is computed from `elf_file.mode()` and `elf_file.owner()` here, but `apply_caps_from_exec()` later calls `set_uid_from_elf()`, which rereads the same mutable inode metadata before applying those precomputed capability sets. If another process changes the executable's setuid bit or owner in between, the new `euid` and the new permitted/effective capability sets are derived from different file states.

**Fix.** Build and apply one exec credential plan from a single inode metadata snapshot. For example, compute the setuid/setgid decisions and `ExecCapSets` together, then have `apply_caps_from_exec()` apply those stored decisions instead of rereading `elf_inode.mode()` and `elf_inode.owner()`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/process/credentials/credentials_.rs` line 470

> ```diff
>         let file_effective = if (!no_root && exec_euid.is_root())
>             || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
>         {
>             CapSet::all()
>         } else {
>             CapSet::empty()
>         };
> ```

Incorrect capability transformation (critical): `file_effective` treats any post-`setuid` root `exec_euid` as enabling all newly permitted capabilities, even when the executable has `security.capability` metadata and the caller's real UID is non-root. Linux's `capabilities(7)` exception for set-user-ID-root programs with file capabilities says the file capability bits are honored in this case, so a file with `F(permitted)` set but no file effective flag should not start with those capabilities in `P'(effective)`. This grants effective capabilities that the file deliberately left non-effective.

**Fix.** Gate the root effective shortcut the same way the root file-capability exception is modeled: when file capabilities are present and `self.ruid()` is non-root, use only `FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag()` for `F(effective)`. For example:

```rust
let grant_root_effective = !no_root
    && exec_euid.is_root()
    && (!has_file_capabilities || self.ruid().is_root());
let file_effective = if grant_root_effective
    || file_capabilities.is_some_and(FileCapabilities::has_effective_flag)
{
    CapSet::all()
} else {
    CapSet::empty()
};
```

---
name: aster-code-review
description: Review code against Asterinas's persona-keyed coding guidelines and write a Markdown review file. Use when asked to review a Git change (diff mode) or a set of target files (files mode) for defects, or inside a write-test-review loop.
---

# aster-code-review

Review code against Asterinas's coding guidelines
— the persona-keyed pages under `book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/`
— and write one Markdown review file.
The review is **recall-first**: miss as few real defects as possible.
It makes both objective calls (undeniable bugs) and subjective calls (guideline violations, each grounded in a cited rule).

There are two review modes, both **anchored at the current checkout (HEAD)**:

- **`diff <base>`** — a Git change:
  the **commit series** the branch adds over its merge-base with `<base>`
  — each commit's message *and* its diff,
  so the review covers per-commit intent and commit hygiene, not just the net change.
  (Commit first; uncommitted edits are not reviewed in diff mode.)
- **`files <path[:lines] ...>`**
  — target code: the working-tree contents of the named files,
  optionally narrowed to line ranges.

To review at a specific commit, check it out first (it becomes HEAD).

This skill is agent-agnostic (Claude Code and Codex).
Only step 3's spawn primitive differs between agents;
everything else is identical.

## Interface

However the skill is triggered,
it receives ONE raw argument string that begins with a mode word:

```
diff   <base>              <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
files  <path[:lines] ...>  <output> [--overwrite] [--per-persona-context=auto|yes|no]
```

- `<base>` (diff) — **required**,
  any ref or SHA;
  reviews the commit series `merge-base(<base>, HEAD)..HEAD` (each commit's message + diff).
- `<path[:lines] ...>` (files) — **required**, one or more targets in the working tree.
  A target is a path, optionally `path:N-M` or `path:N-M,K-L` (1-based, inclusive);
  repeat a path to add ranges.
  Wrap a path in double quotes to allow spaces or colons (`"a: b.rs":10-20`).
- `<output>` — **required, last positional** (`cp src... dest` style);
  refuse to overwrite unless `--overwrite`.
- `--overwrite` — replace the output file if it already exists.
- `--per-persona-context` — `yes`: fan out one isolated agent per persona (best recall).
  `no`: one combined agent reviews all personas (cheaper, lower recall).
  `auto` (default) currently resolves to `yes`;
  it will become a benchmark-driven heuristic.
  Controls step 3 only.

**Do not pre-split the argument string yourself.**
Pass it verbatim, as a single quoted argument,
to `resolve_target.sh` (step 1)
— the script self-tokenizes (whitespace-split except inside double quotes),
which keeps the parse deterministic and identical across agents.

## Pipeline

Run these steps in order.
Steps 1 and 5 are deterministic scripts;
steps 6–8 use the model.
Create one fresh temporary directory for the whole run with `mktemp -d`
and put the resolved input, meta file, pass prompts, and fragment directory under it.
Do not use fixed paths such as `/tmp/aster-review-frags`;
stale fragments are worse than a hard failure.

The helper scripts live in the skill's own `scripts/` directory,
at `.agents/skills/aster-code-review/scripts/` inside the repository under review.
**Invoke every script by its absolute path**
— the working directory is the code under review (the repo root), *not* the skill directory,
so a bare `scripts/…` is not found (notably on Codex, which runs commands from the repo root; do not `cd` into the skill dir either — `resolve_target.sh` needs the repo as its cwd).
Shells do not persist between commands,
so **set `SKILL` at the start of each command that uses it** (or inline the `$(git rev-parse …)`):

```sh
SKILL="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/.agents/skills/aster-code-review"; "$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'
```

The steps below write `$SKILL/scripts/…` as shorthand for that absolute path.

1. **Resolve the target.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" '<raw args>'` prints the canonical review input
   — the commit series (each commit's message + diff) in `diff` mode,
   annotated file excerpts in `files` mode;
   save it to a temp file.
   Run it again with `--meta` first (`"$SKILL/scripts/resolve_target.sh" --meta '<raw args>'`) to get
   `mode=`, `base=`/`files=`, `head=`, `branch=`, `output=`, `overwrite=`, `per_persona_context=`;
   add `date=` (today) and an optional `title=` to make the meta file.
   Pass the raw argument string as a single quoted argument
   so the script's tokenizer sees it intact.
2. **Activate personas.**
   Pick which of the five personas run,
   from the reviewed paths — changed paths in `diff` mode,
   named paths in `files` mode (see *Activation*).
3. **Fan out.**
   Spawn the persona passes (see *Spawning*).
   With `per_persona_context` = `yes` or `auto` (the default),
   run **one isolated PASS per activated persona** — best recall.
   With `no`, run **one combined PASS** over all activated personas in a single context
   — cheaper, lower recall.
   Build each pass's prompt deterministically with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
   — one persona for fan-out, all activated personas for combined
   — and spawn the sub-agent with **that exact text**.
   Each pass returns a JSON array of comments;
   file each under its persona's `<fragdir>/<persona>.json` (the comment's `persona` field says which),
   so step 5 is unchanged.
   The `<fragdir>` must be freshly created for this review,
   and no pass may read or reuse a fragment from an earlier review.
4. **Collect** the per-persona JSON fragments.
5. **Assemble.**
   `"$SKILL/scripts/assemble_review.sh" [--overwrite] <meta> <fragdir> <output>` performs the deterministic merge (group by persona in fixed order, sort by file→line, drop exact duplicates *within a persona*, write frontmatter, leave a `<!-- SUMMARY -->` placeholder).
   Pass `--overwrite` only if the user gave it;
   otherwise the script refuses to clobber an existing `<output>`.
6. **Verify** (see *Verification*).
7. **Consolidate** (see *Consolidation*).
8. **Summary.**
   Replace `<!-- SUMMARY -->` with a constructive summary over the final comments:
   what the code does well, the top issues ranked by severity,
   any structural recommendations.

## Activation

A persona runs unless the reviewed paths *provably* contain nothing in its remit (reviewed paths = changed paths in `diff` mode, named paths in `files` mode):

- **maintainability, development, security** — any reviewed code.
- **hardware** — a reviewed path is assembly (`*.S`, `*.asm`),
  an architecture directory (`.../arch/...`), or contains `asm!` / `global_asm!`.
- **documentation** — a reviewed path is `book/`, any `*.md`,
  syscall-coverage files (`*.scml`), or a user-facing API surface (a syscall or kernel parameter).

Activation is path-based and deterministic;
do not use a model to triage (a wrongly-skipped persona is a silent recall hole).

## Pass contract

The shared reviewer contract
— the review methodology and the JSON comment schema every pass must follow
— lives in [`scripts/pass_contract.md`](scripts/pass_contract.md),
inlined as the **stable head** of every pass prompt by `build_pass_prompt.sh` (step 3).
Do not paraphrase it into the prompt yourself:
retyping it breaks the byte-identical prefix the prompt cache relies on (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).
A pass reads only the persona block(s) it is given (selective exposure),
reviews the **REVIEW INPUT** at the foot of the prompt,
and returns a JSON array of comments per that schema.

## Spawning a pass

Build every pass prompt with `"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input-file> <persona>...`
and spawn the sub-agent with its **exact** output
— only a script keeps the prompt's stable head (contract + guideline) byte-identical across passes and reviews,
which is what lets the prompt cache reuse it (see [`execution_model.md`](spec/execution_model.md)).

**Default (`per_persona_context` = `yes`/`auto`)**
— one pass per activated persona,
each in a CLEAN context with only its own persona block (selective exposure):

- **Claude Code** — spawn a Task sub-agent per persona.
- **Codex** — write each prompt to a file and run the pass with
  `--output-last-message`/`-o`, for example:

  ```sh
  timeout "${ACR_PASS_TIMEOUT_SECONDS:-600}" \
    codex exec -C "$repo" --sandbox read-only \
      -o "$fragdir/$persona.json" \
      - < "$promptdir/$persona.txt" \
      > "$logdir/$persona.log" 2>&1
  ```

  Do **not** redirect `codex exec` stdout to the fragment file;
  stdout contains session logs as well as the final answer.
  The fragment file must contain only the model's final JSON array.
  Validate every fragment with `python3 -m json.tool` before assembly.
  If a pass times out or validation fails, fail closed instead of assembling a
  review from missing, logged, or stale fragments.

**Never spawn a pass by re-running `aster_code_review.sh` / `run_agent.sh`,
nor by re-issuing the skill's own arguments** (e.g. `codex exec … diff <base> <out>`).
A pass is a *reviewer* invocation whose input is the `build_pass_prompt.sh` text
— not another run of this skill.
Re-entering the launcher spawns another orchestrator that spawns another … , an infinite fork bomb;
the launcher now refuses it (`ACR_AGENT_RUNNING`).

Passes are independent;
order does not matter.
Collect each pass's JSON.

**Combined (`per_persona_context` = `no`)**
— build ONE prompt with all activated personas (`"$SKILL/scripts/build_pass_prompt.sh" <input> <p1> <p2> ...`)
and run a single pass;
it reviews through every lens at once and tags each comment with its `persona`.
The input and spawn overhead are paid once,
so it is cheaper
— but a larger shared context dilutes focus,
so it finds fewer defects.
The default stays fan-out:
recall comes first,
and `no` is an explicit, opt-in concession.

## Verification (step 6)

For each comment, isolate the key premise it rests on
— especially an external-system fact (Linux/POSIX behaviour, the System V ABI, Rust semantics).
Try to **refute** it:
re-read the cited code and consult an authoritative source.
For guideline findings,
verify against the persona template and the inlined/bundled guideline root used to build the pass prompt.
Do not retract a finding merely because the historical worktree's `book/` copy has older or narrower wording
when the persona template explicitly instructs the check.
Assign a verdict:

- **confirmed** — keep the comment unchanged.
- **uncertain** — keep it, but prefix `problem` with `(unverified) `.
- **refuted** — remove the comment,
  and append it to a `## Retracted by verification` list at the foot of the file with a one-line reason.

Remove **only** on confident refutation;
an unsure check is `uncertain`, not `refuted`.
This is the only step that may remove a comment, and only false positives.

## Consolidation (step 7)

Find clusters of comments that share one root cause or one fix
(e.g. several manual lock/unlock pairs that all want RAII).
For each cluster, write a single unified fix
and repoint each member's `**Fix.**` at it ("Shared with the other `raii` comments: …").
**Never remove a comment**
— every symptom stays at its own location.

## Output format

The assembled file is:
frontmatter (`date`, `mode`, `base` or `files`, `head`, `branch`, optional `title`),
a `# Summary`,
then a `## <section>` per persona in the order **Maintainability, Correctness, Security, Hardware, Documentation** (Correctness is the Development persona's section).

Each comment is a `### \`file\` line N` subsection:
a quoted ` ```diff ` snippet,
then a prose line `` `<grounding>` (<severity>): <problem> ``,
then a `**Fix.**` paragraph.
Do not restructure what the script emits;
only fill the summary and apply the verify/consolidate edits.

/bin/bash -lc "sed -n '1,220p' kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs" in /tmp/aster-code-review-benchmark-final-20260729T124619Z/work/wt3
 succeeded in 0ms:
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

use core::sync::atomic::Ordering;

use super::SyscallReturn;
use crate::{
    prelude::*,
    process::signal::{
        constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
        sig_mask::SigMask,
    },
};

pub fn sys_rt_sigprocmask(
    how: u32,
    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    sigset_size: usize,
    ctx: &Context,
) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
    debug!(
        "mask op = {:?}, set_ptr = 0x{:x}, oldset_ptr = 0x{:x}, sigset_size = {}",
        mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, sigset_size
    );
    if sigset_size != 8 {
        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
    }
    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
}

fn do_rt_sigprocmask(
    mask_op: MaskOp,
    set_ptr: Vaddr,
    oldset_ptr: Vaddr,
    ctx: &Context,
) -> Result<()> {
    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
        ctx.user_space()
            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
    }

    let sig_mask_ref = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask();
    if set_ptr != 0 {
        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
        match mask_op {
            MaskOp::Block => {
                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
            }
            MaskOp::Unblock => {
                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
            }
            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
        }
    }
    debug!("new set = {:x?}", sig_mask_ref.load(Ordering::Relaxed));

    Ok(())
}

#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
#[repr(u32)]
pub enum MaskOp {
    Block = 0,
    Unblock = 1,
    SetMask = 2,
}

    pub fn contains(&self, other: impl Into<Self>) -> bool {
        let other = other.into();
        self.bits & other.bits == other.bits
    }

    pub fn intersects(&self, other: impl Into<Self>) -> bool {
        let other = other.into();
        self.bits & other.bits != 0
    }

# Summary

<!-- SUMMARY -->

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 9

> ```diff
>     process::signal::{
>         constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
>         sig_mask::SigMask,
> ...
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`qualified-fn-imports` (minor): `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are constants imported directly from `process::signal::constants`, so the later uses look local and lose the module context that the Rust-specific guideline asks call sites to preserve.

**Fix.** Import the parent module and qualify the constants at use sites, e.g. `use crate::process::signal::{constants, sig_mask::SigMask};` and then `constants::SIGKILL` / `constants::SIGSTOP`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): `8` encodes the Linux `rt_sigprocmask` signal-set ABI size, and the same literal appears in related signal syscalls, so readers have to infer the external contract from repeated bare numbers.

**Fix.** Use a named constant, preferably shared with the other signal syscall handlers, such as `const RT_SIGSET_SIZE: usize = core::mem::size_of::<SigMask>();`, and compare `sigset_size` against that name.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`backtick-identifiers` (nit): The code-oriented comment mentions `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` as plain prose, so the constants are not visually distinguished from the surrounding sentence.

**Fix.** Format the identifiers as code, e.g. `attempts to block `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The comment implements Linux-specified behavior but cites only vague `man pages`, leaving the next reader to rediscover which contract requires silently ignoring attempts to block these signals.

**Fix.** Name the specific source, for example `Linux sigprocmask(2)`, and include the URL if that is the local convention.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 68

> ```diff
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
> #[repr(u32)]
> pub enum MaskOp {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `#[derive(...)]` appears before `#[repr(u32)]`, and the derive list is not alphabetically sorted, which diverges from the attribute ordering convention.

**Fix.** Put `#[repr(u32)]` first and sort the derived traits: `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, Eq, PartialEq, TryFromInt)]`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 70

> ```diff
> pub enum MaskOp {
>     Block = 0,
>     Unblock = 1,
>     SetMask = 2,
> }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MaskOp` is declared `pub`, but local search shows it is only used inside `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs`, so the file exposes an implementation detail without an actual consumer.

**Fix.** Make `MaskOp` private, or use a narrower visibility such as `pub(super)` only if a parent module really needs to name it.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 21

> ```diff
> 20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
> 21	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
> 22	    debug!(
> ...
> 29	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall semantics (major): `sys_rt_sigprocmask()` converts `how` to `MaskOp` before checking whether `set_ptr` is `0`. For `rt_sigprocmask`, when `set` is `NULL`, `how` is ignored and the call is only a query of the current mask. A raw call such as `rt_sigprocmask(999, NULL, oldset, 8)` should succeed and fill `oldset`, but this returns `EINVAL` before reaching the query path.

**Fix.** Defer `MaskOp::try_from(how)` until `set_ptr != 0`, or pass `how` into `do_rt_sigprocmask()` and convert it only inside the update branch.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 39	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
> 40	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
> 41	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
> 42	        ctx.user_space()
> 43	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
> 44	    }
> ...
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Pointer aliasing (major): `do_rt_sigprocmask()` writes `oldset_ptr` before copying the new mask from `set_ptr`. If a raw syscall passes the same userspace address for both pointers, the write at `oldset_ptr` overwrites the requested new mask before `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)` reads it. For example, with an empty current mask and a user buffer containing `SIGUSR1`, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, buf, buf, 8)` leaves the thread mask empty instead of blocking `SIGUSR1`.

**Fix.** Copy and validate the new `SigMask` from `set_ptr` into a local before writing `oldset_ptr`; then write the old mask and apply the already-copied new mask.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47	    if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49	        match mask_op {
> 50	            MaskOp::Block => {
> 51	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56	            }
> ...
> 60	            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal mask (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. Attempts to block those signals must be silently ignored for all mask-setting operations, not only `SIG_BLOCK`; with this code, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, {SIGKILL}, NULL, 8)` can make `SIGKILL` remain pending because signal delivery skips signals contained in the thread mask.

**Fix.** Remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying either `MaskOp::Block` or `MaskOp::SetMask`, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47     if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49         match mask_op {
> 50             MaskOp::Block => {
> 51                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56             }
> 57             MaskOp::Unblock => {
> 58                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
> 59             }
> 60             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` only for `MaskOp::Block`, but `MaskOp::SetMask` installs the user-supplied `read_mask` unchanged. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, ...)` with those bits set and make `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` blocked in its thread mask, bypassing the Linux rule that attempts to block `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored.

**Fix.** Clear `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation, or at least before the `MaskOp::SetMask` store, so every path that can block signals enforces the boundary rule.

codex
The assembled review currently has six maintainability comments, three correctness comments, one security comment, and empty documentation. I’m now verifying the external Linux semantics and local signal-mask behavior so false positives can be retracted or marked unverified.

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

//! Signal sets and atomic masks.
//!
//! A signal set is a bit-set of signals. A signal mask is a set of signals
//! that are blocked from delivery to a thread. An atomic signal mask
//! implementation is provided for shared access to signal masks.

use core::{
    fmt::LowerHex,
    ops,
    sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering},
};

use atomic_integer_wrapper::define_atomic_version_of_integer_like_type;

use super::{constants::MIN_STD_SIG_NUM, sig_num::SigNum};
use crate::prelude::*;

/// A signal mask.
///
/// This is an alias to the [`SigSet`]. All the signal in the set are blocked
/// from the delivery to a thread.
pub type SigMask = SigSet;

/// A bit-set of signals.
///
/// Because that all the signal numbers are in the range of 1 to 64, casting
/// a signal set from `u64` to `SigSet` will always succeed.
#[derive(Debug, Copy, Clone, Default, PartialEq, Eq, Pod)]
#[repr(C)]
pub struct SigSet {
    bits: u64,
}

impl From<SigNum> for SigSet {
    fn from(signum: SigNum) -> Self {
        let idx = signum.as_u8() - MIN_STD_SIG_NUM;
        Self { bits: 1_u64 << idx }
    }
}

impl From<u64> for SigSet {
    fn from(bits: u64) -> Self {
        SigSet { bits }
    }
}

impl From<SigSet> for u64 {
    fn from(set: SigSet) -> u64 {
        set.bits
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::BitAnd<T> for SigSet {
    type Output = Self;

    fn bitand(self, rhs: T) -> Self {
        SigSet {
            bits: self.bits & rhs.into().bits,
        }
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::BitAndAssign<T> for SigSet {
    fn bitand_assign(&mut self, rhs: T) {
        self.bits &= rhs.into().bits;
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::BitOr<T> for SigSet {
    type Output = Self;

    fn bitor(self, rhs: T) -> Self {
        SigSet {
            bits: self.bits | rhs.into().bits,
        }
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::BitOrAssign<T> for SigSet {
    fn bitor_assign(&mut self, rhs: T) {
        self.bits |= rhs.into().bits;
    }
}

#[expect(clippy::suspicious_arithmetic_impl)]
impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::Add<T> for SigSet {
    type Output = Self;

    fn add(self, rhs: T) -> Self {
        SigSet {
            bits: self.bits | rhs.into().bits,
        }
    }
}

#[expect(clippy::suspicious_op_assign_impl)]
impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::AddAssign<T> for SigSet {
    fn add_assign(&mut self, rhs: T) {
        self.bits |= rhs.into().bits;
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::Sub<T> for SigSet {
    type Output = Self;

    fn sub(self, rhs: T) -> Self {
        SigSet {
            bits: self.bits & !rhs.into().bits,
        }
    }
}

impl<T: Into<SigSet>> ops::SubAssign<T> for SigSet {
    fn sub_assign(&mut self, rhs: T) {
        self.bits &= !rhs.into().bits;
    }
}

impl ops::Not for SigSet {
    type Output = Self;

    fn not(self) -> Self {
        SigSet { bits: !self.bits }
    }
}

impl SigSet {
    pub fn new_empty() -> Self {
        SigSet { bits: 0 }
    }

    pub fn new_full() -> Self {
        SigSet { bits: !0 }
    }

    pub const fn is_empty(&self) -> bool {
        self.bits == 0
    }

    pub const fn is_full(&self) -> bool {
        self.bits == !0
    }

    pub fn count(&self) -> usize {
        self.bits.count_ones() as usize
    }

    pub fn contains(&self, other: impl Into<Self>) -> bool {
        let other = other.into();
        self.bits & other.bits == other.bits
    }

    pub fn intersects(&self, other: impl Into<Self>) -> bool {
        let other = other.into();
        self.bits & other.bits != 0
    }
}

// This is to allow hexadecimally formatting a `SigSet` when debug printing it.
impl LowerHex for SigSet {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut core::fmt::Formatter<'_>) -> core::fmt::Result {
        LowerHex::fmt(&self.bits, f) // delegate to u64's implementation
    }
}

/// An atomic signal mask.
///
/// This is an alias to the [`AtomicSigSet`]. All the signal in the set are
/// blocked from the delivery to a thread.
///
/// [`Relaxed`]: core::sync::atomic::Ordering::Relaxed
pub type AtomicSigMask = AtomicSigSet;

define_atomic_version_of_integer_like_type!(SigSet, {
    pub struct AtomicSigSet(AtomicU64);
});

impl From<SigSet> for AtomicSigSet {
    fn from(set: SigSet) -> Self {
        Self::new(set)
    }
}

impl AtomicSigSet {
    pub fn new_empty() -> Self {
        AtomicSigSet::new(0)
    }

    pub fn new_full() -> Self {
        AtomicSigSet::new(!0)
    }

    pub fn contains(&self, signals: impl Into<SigSet>, ordering: Ordering) -> bool {
        self.load(ordering).contains(signals.into())
    }
}
                // If there is already a signal pending, just ignore all subsequent signals
                return false;
            }
            *queue = Some(signal);
        } else {
            // Real-time signals
            let queue = self.get_rt_queue_mut(signum);
            queue.push_back(signal);
        }

        true
    }

    fn dequeue(&mut self, blocked: &SigMask) -> Option<Box<dyn Signal>> {
        // Deliver standard signals.
        //
        // According to signal(7):
        // If both standard and real-time signals are pending for a process,
        // POSIX leaves it unspecified which is delivered first. Linux, like
        // many other implementations, gives priority to standard signals in
        // this case.

        // POSIX leaves unspecified which to deliver first if there are multiple
        // pending standard signals. So we are free to define our own. The
        // principle is to give more urgent signals higher priority (like SIGKILL).

        // FIXME: the gvisor pty_test JobControlTest::ReleaseTTY requires that
        // the SIGHUP signal should be handled before SIGCONT.
        const ORDERED_STD_SIGS: [SigNum; COUNT_STD_SIGS] = [
            SIGKILL, SIGTERM, SIGSTOP, SIGSEGV, SIGILL, SIGHUP, SIGCONT, SIGINT, SIGQUIT, SIGTRAP,
            SIGABRT, SIGBUS, SIGFPE, SIGUSR1, SIGUSR2, SIGPIPE, SIGALRM, SIGSTKFLT, SIGCHLD,
            SIGTSTP, SIGTTIN, SIGTTOU, SIGURG, SIGXCPU, SIGXFSZ, SIGVTALRM, SIGPROF, SIGWINCH,
            SIGIO, SIGPWR, SIGSYS,
        ];
        for &signum in &ORDERED_STD_SIGS {
            if blocked.contains(signum) {
                continue;
            }

            let queue = self.get_std_queue_mut(signum);
            let signal = queue.take();
            if signal.is_some() {
                return signal;
            }
        }

        // If no standard signals, then deliver real-time signals.
        //
        // According to signal (7):
        // Real-time signals are delivered in a guaranteed order.  Multiple
        // real-time signals of the same type are delivered in the order
        // they were sent.  If different real-time signals are sent to a
        // process, they are delivered starting with the lowest-numbered
        // signal.  (I.e., low-numbered signals have highest priority.)
        for signum in MIN_RT_SIG_NUM..=MAX_RT_SIG_NUM {
            let signum = SigNum::try_from(signum).unwrap();
            if blocked.contains(signum) {
                continue;
            }

            let queue = self.get_rt_queue_mut(signum);
            let signal = queue.pop_front();
            if signal.is_some() {
                return signal;
            }
        }

        // There must be pending but blocked signals
        None
    }

    /// Returns whether the `SigQueues` has some pending signals which are not blocked
    fn has_pending(&self, blocked: SigMask) -> bool {
        self.std_queues.iter().any(|signal| {
            signal
                .as_ref()
                .is_some_and(|signal| !blocked.contains(signal.num()))
        }) || self.rt_queues.iter().any(|rt_queue| !rt_queue.is_empty())
    }

    fn get_std_queue_mut(&mut self, signum: SigNum) -> &mut Option<Box<dyn Signal>> {
        debug_assert!(signum.is_std());
        let idx = (signum.as_u8() - MIN_STD_SIG_NUM) as usize;
        &mut self.std_queues[idx]
    }

    fn get_rt_queue_mut(&mut self, signum: SigNum) -> &mut VecDeque<Box<dyn Signal>> {
        debug_assert!(signum.is_real_time());
        let idx = (signum.as_u8() - MIN_RT_SIG_NUM) as usize;
        &mut self.rt_queues[idx]
    }

    fn sig_pending(&self) -> SigSet {
        let mut pending = SigSet::new_empty();

define_std_signums! {
    SIGHUP    = 1, // Hangup detected on controlling terminal or death of controlling process
    SIGINT    = 2, // Interrupt from keyboard
    SIGQUIT   = 3, // Quit from keyboard
    SIGILL    = 4, // Illegal Instruction
    SIGTRAP   = 5, // Trace/breakpoint trap
    SIGABRT   = 6, // Abort signal from abort(3)
    SIGBUS    = 7, // Bus error (bad memory access)
    SIGFPE    = 8, // Floating-point exception
    SIGKILL   = 9, // Kill signal
    SIGUSR1   = 10, // User-defined signal 1
    SIGSEGV   = 11, // Invalid memory reference
    SIGUSR2   = 12, // User-defined signal 2
    SIGPIPE   = 13, // Broken pipe: write to pipe with no readers; see pipe(7)
    SIGALRM   = 14, // Timer signal from alarm(2)
    SIGTERM   = 15, // Termination signal
    SIGSTKFLT = 16, // Stack fault on coprocessor (unused)
    SIGCHLD   = 17, // Child stopped or terminated
    SIGCONT   = 18, // Continue if stopped
    SIGSTOP   = 19, // Stop process
    SIGTSTP   = 20, // Stop typed at terminal
    SIGTTIN   = 21, // Terminal input for background process
    SIGTTOU   = 22, // Terminal output for background process
    SIGURG    = 23, // Urgent condition on socket (4.2BSD)
    SIGXCPU   = 24, // CPU time limit exceeded (4.2BSD); see setrlimit(2)
    SIGXFSZ   = 25, // File size limit exceeded (4.2BSD); see setrlimit(2)
    SIGVTALRM = 26, // Virtual alarm clock (4.2BSD)
    SIGPROF   = 27, // Profiling timer expired
    SIGWINCH  = 28, // Window resize signal (4.3BSD, Sun)
    SIGIO     = 29, // I/O now possible (4.2BSD)
    SIGPWR    = 30, // Power failure (System V)
    SIGSYS    = 31, // Bad system call (SVr4); see also seccomp(2)
}

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The syscall implementation is small and easy to follow, and the basic `SIG_BLOCK` / `SIG_UNBLOCK` paths reuse the existing `SigMask` operations cleanly. The main risks are in Linux compatibility edge cases around raw syscall inputs.
+
+The highest-impact issue is that `SIG_SETMASK` can install `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` into the blocked mask; this breaks the Linux rule that attempts to block those signals are silently ignored and can affect signal delivery. The next correctness issue is that `how` is validated even when `set` is `NULL`, although Linux ignores `how` in query-only calls. The user-buffer aliasing between `set` and `oldset` is also worth fixing by reading the new mask before writing the old one.
+
+The remaining findings are maintainability items: naming the ABI sigset size, narrowing `MaskOp` visibility, preserving signal-constant context at call sites, and tightening the comment/source style.
 
 ## Maintainability
 
@@ -140,7 +144,7 @@
 
 Incorrect signal mask (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. Attempts to block those signals must be silently ignored for all mask-setting operations, not only `SIG_BLOCK`; with this code, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, {SIGKILL}, NULL, 8)` can make `SIGKILL` remain pending because signal delivery skips signals contained in the thread mask.
 
-**Fix.** Remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying either `MaskOp::Block` or `MaskOp::SetMask`, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.
+**Fix.** Shared with the security comment below: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.
 
 ## Security
 
@@ -165,4 +169,4 @@
 
 `validate-at-boundaries` (major): `rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` only for `MaskOp::Block`, but `MaskOp::SetMask` installs the user-supplied `read_mask` unchanged. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, ...)` with those bits set and make `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` blocked in its thread mask, bypassing the Linux rule that attempts to block `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored.
 
-**Fix.** Clear `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation, or at least before the `MaskOp::SetMask` store, so every path that can block signals enforces the boundary rule.
+**Fix.** Shared with the correctness comment above: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.

-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The syscall implementation is small and easy to follow, and the basic `SIG_BLOCK` / `SIG_UNBLOCK` paths reuse the existing `SigMask` operations cleanly. The main risks are in Linux compatibility edge cases around raw syscall inputs.
+
+The highest-impact issue is that `SIG_SETMASK` can install `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` into the blocked mask; this breaks the Linux rule that attempts to block those signals are silently ignored and can affect signal delivery. The next correctness issue is that `how` is validated even when `set` is `NULL`, although Linux ignores `how` in query-only calls. title: "Review of rt_sigprocmask syscall"
---

# Summary

The syscall implementation is small and easy to follow, and the basic `SIG_BLOCK` / `SIG_UNBLOCK` paths reuse the existing `SigMask` operations cleanly. The main risks are in Linux compatibility edge cases around raw syscall inputs.

The highest-impact issue is that `SIG_SETMASK` can install `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` into the blocked mask; this breaks the Linux rule that attempts to block those signals are silently ignored and can affect signal delivery. The next correctness issue is that `how` is validated even when `set` is `NULL`, although Linux ignores `how` in query-only calls. The user-buffer aliasing between `set` and `oldset` is also worth fixing by reading the new mask before writing the old one.

The remaining findings are maintainability items: naming the ABI sigset size, narrowing `MaskOp` visibility, preserving signal-constant context at call sites, and tightening the comment/source style.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 9

> ```diff
>     process::signal::{
>         constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
>         sig_mask::SigMask,
> ...
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`qualified-fn-imports` (minor): `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are constants imported directly from `process::signal::constants`, so the later uses look local and lose the module context that the Rust-specific guideline asks call sites to preserve.

**Fix.** Import the parent module and qualify the constants at use sites, e.g. `use crate::process::signal::{constants, sig_mask::SigMask};` and then `constants::SIGKILL` / `constants::SIGSTOP`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): `8` encodes the Linux `rt_sigprocmask` signal-set ABI size, and the same literal appears in related signal syscalls, so readers have to infer the external contract from repeated bare numbers.

**Fix.** Use a named constant, preferably shared with the other signal syscall handlers, such as `const RT_SIGSET_SIZE: usize = core::mem::size_of::<SigMask>();`, and compare `sigset_size` against that name.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`backtick-identifiers` (nit): The code-oriented comment mentions `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` as plain prose, so the constants are not visually distinguished from the surrounding sentence.

**Fix.** Format the identifiers as code, e.g. `attempts to block `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The comment implements Linux-specified behavior but cites only vague `man pages`, leaving the next reader to rediscover which contract requires silently ignoring attempts to block these signals.

**Fix.** Name the specific source, for example `Linux sigprocmask(2)`, and include the URL if that is the local convention.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 68

> ```diff
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
> #[repr(u32)]
> pub enum MaskOp {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `#[derive(...)]` appears before `#[repr(u32)]`, and the derive list is not alphabetically sorted, which diverges from the attribute ordering convention.

**Fix.** Put `#[repr(u32)]` first and sort the derived traits: `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, Eq, PartialEq, TryFromInt)]`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 70

> ```diff
> pub enum MaskOp {
>     Block = 0,
>     Unblock = 1,
>     SetMask = 2,
> }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MaskOp` is declared `pub`, but local search shows it is only used inside `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs`, so the file exposes an implementation detail without an actual consumer.

**Fix.** Make `MaskOp` private, or use a narrower visibility such as `pub(super)` only if a parent module really needs to name it.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 21

> ```diff
> 20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
> 21	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
> 22	    debug!(
> ...
> 29	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall semantics (major): `sys_rt_sigprocmask()` converts `how` to `MaskOp` before checking whether `set_ptr` is `0`. For `rt_sigprocmask`, when `set` is `NULL`, `how` is ignored and the call is only a query of the current mask. A raw call such as `rt_sigprocmask(999, NULL, oldset, 8)` should succeed and fill `oldset`, but this returns `EINVAL` before reaching the query path.

**Fix.** Defer `MaskOp::try_from(how)` until `set_ptr != 0`, or pass `how` into `do_rt_sigprocmask()` and convert it only inside the update branch.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 39	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
> 40	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
> 41	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
> 42	        ctx.user_space()
> 43	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
> 44	    }
> ...
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Pointer aliasing (major): `do_rt_sigprocmask()` writes `oldset_ptr` before copying the new mask from `set_ptr`. If a raw syscall passes the same userspace address for both pointers, the write at `oldset_ptr` overwrites the requested new mask before `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)` reads it. For example, with an empty current mask and a user buffer containing `SIGUSR1`, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, buf, buf, 8)` leaves the thread mask empty instead of blocking `SIGUSR1`.

**Fix.** Copy and validate the new `SigMask` from `set_ptr` into a local before writing `oldset_ptr`; then write the old mask and apply the already-copied new mask.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47	    if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49	        match mask_op {
> 50	            MaskOp::Block => {
> 51	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56	            }
> ...
> 60	            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal mask (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. Attempts to block those signals must be silently ignored for all mask-setting operations, not only `SIG_BLOCK`; with this code, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, {SIGKILL}, NULL, 8)` can make `SIGKILL` remain pending because signal delivery skips signals contained in the thread mask.

**Fix.** Shared with the security comment below: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47     if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49         match mask_op {
> 50             MaskOp::Block => {
> 51                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56             }
> 57             MaskOp::Unblock => {
> 58                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
> 59             }
> 60             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` only for `MaskOp::Block`, but `MaskOp::SetMask` installs the user-supplied `read_mask` unchanged. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, ...)` with those bits set and make `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` blocked in its thread mask, bypassing the Linux rule that attempts to block `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness comment above: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.

# Summary

The syscall implementation is small and easy to follow, and the basic `SIG_BLOCK` / `SIG_UNBLOCK` paths reuse the existing `SigMask` operations cleanly. The main risks are in Linux compatibility edge cases around raw syscall inputs.

The highest-impact issue is that `SIG_SETMASK` can install `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` into the blocked mask; this breaks the Linux rule that attempts to block those signals are silently ignored and can affect signal delivery. The next correctness issue is that `how` is validated even when `set` is `NULL`, although Linux ignores `how` in query-only calls. The user-buffer aliasing between `set` and `oldset` is also worth fixing by reading the new mask before writing the old one.

The remaining findings are maintainability items: naming the ABI sigset size, narrowing `MaskOp` visibility, preserving signal-constant context at call sites, and tightening the comment/source style.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 9

> ```diff
>     process::signal::{
>         constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
>         sig_mask::SigMask,
> ...
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`qualified-fn-imports` (minor): `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are constants imported directly from `process::signal::constants`, so the later uses look local and lose the module context that the Rust-specific guideline asks call sites to preserve.

**Fix.** Import the parent module and qualify the constants at use sites, e.g. `use crate::process::signal::{constants, sig_mask::SigMask};` and then `constants::SIGKILL` / `constants::SIGSTOP`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }

===== EXPECTED DEFECTS =====
# Expected defects

1. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: development, grounding: Incorrect signal semantics, severity: critical)
   defect: `sys_rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `read_mask` for `MaskOp::Block`, but the `MaskOp::SetMask` arm stores the user-provided mask verbatim with `sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)`. A caller using `SIG_SETMASK` can therefore place `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` in the thread's blocked signal mask. Linux requires attempts to block these two signals to be silently ignored, so accepting them in the set-mask path can make user tasks unkillable or unstoppable and breaks signal semantics.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that every path installing a blocked signal mask, including `SIG_SETMASK`, must preserve the invariant that `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` cannot be blocked.

2. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: development, grounding: Incorrect argument validation, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_rt_sigprocmask` converts `how` with `MaskOp::try_from(how)?` before it checks whether `set_ptr` is null. Linux ignores the `how` argument when `set` is `NULL`, because that call only reads the current signal mask through `oldset`. A call such as `rt_sigprocmask(999, NULL, oldset, 8)` should therefore succeed and report the old mask, but this implementation returns `EINVAL` before it reaches the `oldset_ptr` write.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that `rt_sigprocmask` must ignore invalid `how` values when `set` is `NULL`; only calls that install or change a signal mask should validate the operation.

3. location: kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs (persona: maintainability, grounding: narrow-visibility, severity: minor)
   defect: `MaskOp` is declared `pub`, but it is only used inside `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` to decode the syscall's `how` argument. Exposing this syscall-local enum creates an unnecessary module interface for an implementation detail that no other module needs to name.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that syscall-local decode enums should not be public when all uses are contained in the same source file.

title: "Review of rt_sigprocmask syscall"
