# Summary

The syscall implementation is small and easy to follow, and the basic `SIG_BLOCK` / `SIG_UNBLOCK` paths reuse the existing `SigMask` operations cleanly. The main risks are in Linux compatibility edge cases around raw syscall inputs.

The highest-impact issue is that `SIG_SETMASK` can install `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` into the blocked mask; this breaks the Linux rule that attempts to block those signals are silently ignored and can affect signal delivery. The next correctness issue is that `how` is validated even when `set` is `NULL`, although Linux ignores `how` in query-only calls. The user-buffer aliasing between `set` and `oldset` is also worth fixing by reading the new mask before writing the old one.

The remaining findings are maintainability items: naming the ABI sigset size, narrowing `MaskOp` visibility, preserving signal-constant context at call sites, and tightening the comment/source style.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 9

> ```diff
>     process::signal::{
>         constants::{SIGKILL, SIGSTOP},
>         sig_mask::SigMask,
> ...
>                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
>                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> ```

`qualified-fn-imports` (minor): `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are constants imported directly from `process::signal::constants`, so the later uses look local and lose the module context that the Rust-specific guideline asks call sites to preserve.

**Fix.** Import the parent module and qualify the constants at use sites, e.g. `use crate::process::signal::{constants, sig_mask::SigMask};` and then `constants::SIGKILL` / `constants::SIGSTOP`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 26

> ```diff
>     if sigset_size != 8 {
>         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "sigset size is not equal to 8");
>     }
> ```

`no-magic-number` (minor): `8` encodes the Linux `rt_sigprocmask` signal-set ABI size, and the same literal appears in related signal syscalls, so readers have to infer the external contract from repeated bare numbers.

**Fix.** Use a named constant, preferably shared with the other signal syscall handlers, such as `const RT_SIGSET_SIZE: usize = core::mem::size_of::<SigMask>();`, and compare `sigset_size` against that name.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`backtick-identifiers` (nit): The code-oriented comment mentions `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` as plain prose, so the constants are not visually distinguished from the surrounding sentence.

**Fix.** Format the identifiers as code, e.g. "attempts to block `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored".

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 51

> ```diff
>                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
>                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> ```

`cite-sources` (minor): The comment implements Linux-specified behavior but cites only vague `man pages`, leaving the next reader to rediscover which contract requires silently ignoring attempts to block these signals.

**Fix.** Name the specific source, for example `Linux sigprocmask(2)`, and include the URL if that is the local convention.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 68

> ```diff
> #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, TryFromInt)]
> #[repr(u32)]
> pub enum MaskOp {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): `#[derive(...)]` appears before `#[repr(u32)]`, and the derive list is not alphabetically sorted, which diverges from the attribute ordering convention.

**Fix.** Put `#[repr(u32)]` first and sort the derived traits: `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, Eq, PartialEq, TryFromInt)]`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 70

> ```diff
> pub enum MaskOp {
>     Block = 0,
>     Unblock = 1,
>     SetMask = 2,
> }
> ```

`narrow-visibility` (minor): `MaskOp` is declared `pub`, but local search shows it is only used inside `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs`, so the file exposes an implementation detail without an actual consumer.

**Fix.** Make `MaskOp` private, or use a narrower visibility such as `pub(super)` only if a parent module really needs to name it.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 21

> ```diff
> 20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
> 21	    let mask_op = MaskOp::try_from(how)?;
> 22	    debug!(
> ...
> 29	    do_rt_sigprocmask(mask_op, set_ptr, oldset_ptr, ctx)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall semantics (major): `sys_rt_sigprocmask()` converts `how` to `MaskOp` before checking whether `set_ptr` is `0`. For `rt_sigprocmask`, when `set` is `NULL`, `how` is ignored and the call is only a query of the current mask. A raw call such as `rt_sigprocmask(999, NULL, oldset, 8)` should succeed and fill `oldset`, but this returns `EINVAL` before reaching the query path.

**Fix.** Defer `MaskOp::try_from(how)` until `set_ptr != 0`, or pass `how` into `do_rt_sigprocmask()` and convert it only inside the update branch.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 39	    let old_sig_mask_value = ctx.posix_thread.sig_mask().load(Ordering::Relaxed);
> 40	    debug!("old sig mask value: 0x{:x}", old_sig_mask_value);
> 41	    if oldset_ptr != 0 {
> 42	        ctx.user_space()
> 43	            .write_val(oldset_ptr, &old_sig_mask_value)?;
> 44	    }
> ...
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> ```

Pointer aliasing (major): `do_rt_sigprocmask()` writes `oldset_ptr` before copying the new mask from `set_ptr`. If a raw syscall passes the same userspace address for both pointers, the write at `oldset_ptr` overwrites the requested new mask before `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)` reads it. For example, with an empty current mask and a user buffer containing `SIGUSR1`, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, buf, buf, 8)` leaves the thread mask empty instead of blocking `SIGUSR1`.

**Fix.** Copy and validate the new `SigMask` from `set_ptr` into a local before writing `oldset_ptr`; then write the old mask and apply the already-copied new mask.

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47	    if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48	        let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49	        match mask_op {
> 50	            MaskOp::Block => {
> 51	                // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52	                // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53	                read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54	                read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55	                sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56	            }
> ...
> 60	            MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

Incorrect signal mask (major): `MaskOp::SetMask` stores `read_mask` without removing `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP`. Attempts to block those signals must be silently ignored for all mask-setting operations, not only `SIG_BLOCK`; with this code, `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, {SIGKILL}, NULL, 8)` can make `SIGKILL` remain pending because signal delivery skips signals contained in the thread mask.

**Fix.** Shared with the security comment below: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/rt_sigprocmask.rs` line 60

> ```diff
> 47     if set_ptr != 0 {
> 48         let mut read_mask = ctx.user_space().read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)?;
> 49         match mask_op {
> 50             MaskOp::Block => {
> 51                 // According to man pages, "it is not possible to block SIGKILL or SIGSTOP.
> 52                 // Attempts to do so are silently ignored."
> 53                 read_mask -= SIGKILL;
> 54                 read_mask -= SIGSTOP;
> 55                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value + read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed);
> 56             }
> 57             MaskOp::Unblock => {
> 58                 sig_mask_ref.store(old_sig_mask_value - read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed)
> 59             }
> 60             MaskOp::SetMask => sig_mask_ref.store(read_mask, Ordering::Relaxed),
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `rt_sigprocmask` sanitizes `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` only for `MaskOp::Block`, but `MaskOp::SetMask` installs the user-supplied `read_mask` unchanged. A process can call `rt_sigprocmask(SIG_SETMASK, ...)` with those bits set and make `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP` blocked in its thread mask, bypassing the Linux rule that attempts to block `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` are silently ignored.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness comment above: remove `SIGKILL` and `SIGSTOP` from the user-supplied mask before applying any operation that can block signals, for example immediately after `read_val::<SigMask>(set_ptr)`.
